>>  #!/usr/bin/env bash
>> # Joker Neon Terminal — pulsing every second
>> BG_BLACK="\033[40m"
>> BRIGHT_PURPLE="${BG_BLACK}\033[1;35m"
>> BRIGHT_GREEN="${BG_BLACK}\033[1;32m"
>> RESET="\033[0m"
>>
>> CLEAR_SCREEN="\033[2J\033[H"
>>
>> # Load ASCII pages
>> JOKER_PAGE_1="$(<../assets/page1.txt)"
>> JOKER_PAGE_2="$(<../assets/page2.txt)"
>> JOKER_PAGE_3="$(<../assets/page3.txt)"
>>
>> print_page_strict_colors() {
>>     local page="$1"
>>     local use_green="$2"
>>     local border_color="$BRIGHT_PURPLE"
>>     local inner_color="$BRIGHT_GREEN"
>>     if [[ "$use_green" == "purple" ]]; then
>>         inner_color="$BRIGHT_PURPLE"
>>         border_color="$BRIGHT_GREEN"
>>     fi
>>     while IFS= read -r line; do
>>         if [[ "${line:0:1}" == "X" && "${line: -1}" == "X" && ${#line} -ge 3 ]]; then
>>             first_char="${line:0:1}"
>>             last_char="${line: -1}"
>>             middle="${line:1:${#line}-2}"
>>             printf "%b" "${border_color}${first_char}${RESET}"
>>             printf "%b" "${inner_color}${BG_BLACK}${middle}${RESET}"
>>             printf "%b\n" "${border_color}${last_char}${RESET}"
>>         else
>>             printf "%b\n" "${inner_color}${BG_BLACK}${line}${RESET}"
>>         fi
>>     done <<< "$page"
>> }
>>
>> run_cycle_for_page() {
>>     local page="$1"
>>     local color_phase="$2"
>>     print_page_strict_colors "$page" "$color_phase"
>>     sleep 1
>>     clear
>>     print_page_strict_colors "$page" "$color_phase"
>>     sleep 1
>>     clear
>> }
>>
>> while true; do
>>     run_cycle_for_page "$JOKER_PAGE_1" "green"
>>     run_cycle_for_page "$JOKER_PAGE_2" "purple"
>>     run_cycle_for_page "$JOKER_PAGE_3" "green"
>> done @echo off
>> :: Joker Neon Terminal (Green/Purple on Black)
>> color 0A
>> :loop
>> type ..\assets\page1.txt
>> timeout /t 1 /nobreak >nul
>> cls
>> type ..\assets\page2.txt
>> timeout /t 1 /nobreak >nul
>> cls
>> type ..\assets\page3.txt
>> timeout /t 1 /nobreak >nul
>> cls
>> goto loop

>> const BRIGHT_GREEN = '\x1b[1;32m';
>> const BRIGHT_PURPLE = '\x1b[1;35m';
>> const BG_BLACK = '\x1b[40m';
>> const RESET = '\x1b[0m';
>>
>> function clearConsole() {
>>   process.stdout.write('\x1b[2J\x1b[H');
>> }
>>
>> function printPage(page, color) {
>>   const borderColor = color === 'green' ? BRIGHT_PURPLE : BRIGHT_GREEN;
>>   const innerColor = color === 'green' ? BRIGHT_GREEN : BRIGHT_PURPLE;
>>   page.split('\n').forEach(line => {
>>     if(line.startsWith('X') && line.endsWith('X') && line.length >= 3){
>>       const first = line[0], last = line[line.length-1], middle = line.slice(1,-1);
>>       process.stdout.write(${borderColor}${first}${RESET}${innerColor}${BG_BLACK}${middle}${RESET}${borderColor}${last}${RESET}\n);
>>     } else {
>>       process.stdout.write(${innerColor}${BG_BLACK}${line}${RESET}\n);
>>     }
>>   });
>> }
>>
>> async function pulse() {
>>   while(true){
>>     for(let i=0;i<pages.length;i++){
>>       printPage(pages[i], i%2===0?'green':'purple');
>>       await new Promise(r=>setTimeout(r,1000));
>>       clearConsole();
>>       printPage(pages[i], i%2===0?'green':'purple');
>>       await new Promise(r=>setTimeout(r,1000));
>>       clearConsole();
>>     }
>>   }
>> }

>> }Replicating a tool like Ludus AI is an ambitious but completely achievable project. Since you want to make this free and open-source, you will essentially be building an "LLM Orchestration Layer" that sits inside the game engine.
>>
>> To do this, you don't need to crack their code; you need to replicate their Architecture.
>>
>> Here is the technical roadmap to building your own "Free Ludus" for Unreal Engine 5 and Unity.
>>
>> 🏗️ The Core Architecture
>> Your plugin needs three distinct parts to function:
>>
>> The Eyes (Context Reader): Scripts that look at what the user has selected (Context Awareness).
>>
>> The Brain (LLM Backend): A connection to a free AI model (like Llama 3 or DeepSeek) running locally or via a cheap API.
>>
>> The Hands (Execution Layer): Scripts that take the AI's text response and turn it into actual Engine commands (Spawning actors, creating scripts).
>>
>> 🔧 Part 1: The Unreal Engine 5 Implementation
>> Unreal's Editor is built on C++, but the easiest way to control it for this purpose is Python. Unreal has a massive "Python Editor Script Plugin" that Ludus likely heavily utilizes.
>>
>> Step 1: Set up "The Eyes" (Python)
>> You need a script that grabs the user's current selection to send to the AI as context. Enable Plugins: Python Editor Script Plugin and Editor Scripting Utilities.
>>
>> Script to Get Context (Python):
>>
>> Python
>>
>> import unreal
>>
>> def get_editor_context():
>>     # 1. Get Selected Actors
>>     editor_system = unreal.get_editor_subsystem(unreal.EditorActorSubsystem)
>>     selected_actors = editor_system.get_selected_level_actors()
>>
>>     context_str = "User currently has selected: "
>>     for actor in selected_actors:
>>         # Get name, location, and class of what is selected
>>         actor_name = actor.get_actor_label()
>>         actor_class = actor.get_class().get_name()
>>         actor_loc = actor.get_actor_location()
>>         context_str += f"\n- {actor_name} (Class: {actor_class}) at {actor_loc}"
>>
>>     return context_str
>> Step 2: Set up "The Hands" (Execution)
>> When the AI says "Make a line of 10 cubes," you need a script to parse that.
>>
>> Script to Execute Actions:
>>
>> Python
>>
>> def spawn_grid_of_cubes(rows, cols):
>>     for i in range(rows):
>>         for j in range(cols):
>>             # Load the Cube asset
>>             cube_asset = unreal.load_asset("/Engine/BasicShapes/Cube.Cube")
>>
>>             # Calculate location
>>             loc = unreal.Vector(i * 100, j * 100, 0)
>>
>>             # Spawn it
>>             unreal.EditorLevelLibrary.spawn_actor_from_object(cube_asset, loc)
>> Step 3: Blueprint Generation (The Hard Part)
>> Generating Blueprints programmatically is difficult. The "Cheat Code" method is to use the Clipboard Format.
>>
>> Concept: When you copy a node in Unreal, it copies as text. If you paste that text, it becomes a node.
>>
>> Strategy: Prompt your AI to "Generate the text format of an Unreal Engine Blueprint Node for [Logic]."
>>
>> Implementation: Your Python script simply takes the AI's output and copies it to the system clipboard. The user then just presses Ctrl+V in their graph.
>>
>> 🎮 Part 2: The Unity Engine Implementation
>> Unity is easier to automate because the Editor API is C#, the same language you use for the game.
>>
>> Step 1: Set up "The Eyes" (C# Editor Script)
>> Create a script in an Editor folder. This will read the Selection.
>>
>> C#
>>
>> using UnityEditor;
>> using UnityEngine;
>>
>> public class AIContextReader
>> {
>>     public static string GetCurrentContext()
>>     {
>>         string context = "Selected Objects:\n";
>>         foreach (GameObject obj in Selection.gameObjects)
>>         {
>>             context += $"- {obj.name} (Pos: {obj.transform.position})\n";
>>             // You can even read components attached to it
>>             foreach(Component c in obj.GetComponents<Component>())
>>             {
>>                 context += $"  -- Has Component: {c.GetType().Name}\n";
>>             }
>>         }
>>         return context;
>>     }
>> }
>> Step 2: Set up "The Hands" (Execution)
>> You need an "Agent" mode that allows the AI to run code. Warning: Security risk. You are letting AI execute code. Sandboxing is hard in C#.
>>
>> The safest way is to have the AI generate a temporary script, save it to Assets/Editor/TempAIAction.cs, wait for Unity to compile it, and then execute a specific method within it.
>>
>> 🧠 Part 3: The Free "Brain" (Local LLM)
>> To make it truly free, you cannot rely on OpenAI's API (which costs money). You must integrate Local LLMs.
>>
>> Ollama: Tell your users to install Ollama. It runs models like Llama 3 or DeepSeek Coder locally for free.
>>
>> The Connection: Your Unreal/Unity plugin will send HTTP POST requests to http://localhost:11434/api/generate.
>>
>> Example HTTP Request (Python for Unreal):
>>
>> Python
>>
>> import requests
>> import json
>>
>> def ask_local_ai(prompt, context):
>>     url = "http://localhost:11434/api/generate"
>>
>>     full_prompt = f"CONTEXT: {context}\n\nUSER REQUEST: {prompt}\n\nWrite a Python script for Unreal Engine to do this."
>>
>>     data = {
>>         "model": "llama3",
>>         "prompt": full_prompt,
>>         "stream": False
>>     }
>>     using UnityEditor;
>> using UnityEngine;
>>
>> public static class AIContextReader
>> {
>>     public static string GetCurrentContext()
>>     {
>>         string context = "Selected Objects:\n";
>>         foreach (GameObject obj in Selection.gameObjects)
>>         {
>>             context += $"- {obj.name} (Pos: {obj.transform.position})\n";
>>             foreach(Component c in obj.GetComponents<Component>())
>>                 context += $"  -- Component: {c.GetType().Name}\n";
>>         }
>>         return context;
>>     }
>> }
>>     response = requests.post(url, json=data)
>>     return response.json()['response']
>> 🚀 Next Steps for You
>> Download "Ollama" and get the "DeepSeek Coder" model (it is excellent at coding).
>>
>> Start with Unreal Python: Open Unreal, go to Output Log, switch it to Python, and try the scripts above to see how they work.
>>
>> Build the UI: In Unreal, use "Editor Utility Widgets" (UMG) to create a simple window with a Text Box (User Input) and a Button (Send to AI).
>>
>> Connect them: When the button is clicked -> Get Context -> Send to Ollama -> Execute the returned Python code.
>>
>> This is the exact loop Ludus uses. If you build this interface and release it on GitHub, you will have successfully created the open-source alternative.

""" # 1. Gather Context current_context = ai_context.get_selected_context() # 2. Construct the full System Prompt system_instruction = ( "You are an Unreal Engine 5 Expert Python Script Generator. >> python). "

>> def run_ai_tool(user_input):

>>     Main function called by the UI.

>>         # 1. Get Code from AI

>>
>>         if not generated_code:
>>             unreal.log_error("AI returned no code.")
>>             return
>>
>>         unreal.log_warning(f"AI Generated Code:\\n{generated_code}")
>>
>>         # 2. Execute Code
>>         slow_task.enter_progress_frame(1, "Executing...")
>>         try:
>>             exec(generated_code, globals())
>>             unreal.log("AI Task Completed Successfully.")
>>         except Exception as e:
>>             unreal.log_error(f"Execution Error: {e}")

ChatGPT said:

Thought for 17s
You want the PowerShell script targeted to E:. Fine. Below is a ready-to-run PowerShell script that:

Checks for admin rights

Creates the E:\OpenSourceLudus scaffold and Content\Python, Unreal, Unity folders

Installs Ollama via winget if missing (attempts it) and pulls deepseek-coder model (if Ollama is present)

Writes three Python helper scripts for Unreal (ai_context.py, ai_client.py, init_unreal.py) and an INSTRUCTIONS.txt file

Opens the INSTRUCTIONS when done

Copy-paste the whole thing into an elevated PowerShell (Run as Administrator) and run it. Try not to break Windows while we’re being useful.

# create_open_sourceludus_e_drive.ps1
# Run as Administrator in PowerShell
# Target: E:\OpenSourceLudus

# -----------------------
# Safety / privileges
# -----------------------
if (-not ([Security.Principal.WindowsPrincipal][Security.Principal.WindowsIdentity]::GetCurrent()).IsInRole([Security.Principal.WindowsBuiltInRole]::Administrator)) {
    Write-Error "Please run this script as Administrator."
    exit 1
}

# -----------------------
# Config
# -----------------------
$TargetDrive   = "E:\"
$ProjectFolder = "OpenSourceLudus"
$BasePath      = Join-Path $TargetDrive $ProjectFolder
$PythonPath    = Join-Path $BasePath "Content\Python"
$UnrealPath    = Join-Path $BasePath "Unreal"
$UnityPath     = Join-Path $BasePath "Unity"
$InstructionsFile = Join-Path $BasePath "INSTRUCTIONS.txt"

# -----------------------
# Create directories
# -----------------------
Write-Host "Creating project scaffold at $BasePath ..." -ForegroundColor Cyan
New-Item -ItemType Directory -Force -Path $PythonPath | Out-Null
New-Item -ItemType Directory -Force -Path $UnrealPath | Out-Null
New-Item -ItemType Directory -Force -Path $UnityPath | Out-Null

# -----------------------
# Install Ollama (if missing)
# -----------------------
function Ensure-Ollama {
    Write-Host "Checking for Ollama..." -ForegroundColor Cyan
    $ollamaCmd = Get-Command "ollama" -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue
    if ($null -ne $ollamaCmd) {
        Write-Host "Ollama is already installed." -ForegroundColor Green
        return $true
    }

    if (-not (Get-Command "winget" -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue)) {
        Write-Warning "winget not found. Please install Ollama manually: https://ollama.com"
        return $false
    }

    Write-Host "Attempting to install Ollama via winget..." -ForegroundColor Yellow
    & winget install -e --id Ollama.Ollama
    Start-Sleep -Seconds 2

    $ollamaCmd = Get-Command "ollama" -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue
    if ($null -ne $ollamaCmd) {
        Write-Host "Ollama installed." -ForegroundColor Green
        return $true
    } else {
        Write-Warning "Failed to install Ollama automatically. Please install it manually from https://ollama.com"
        return $false
    }
}

$ollamaAvailable = Ensure-Ollama

# -----------------------
# Pull recommended model (if Ollama available)
# -----------------------
if ($ollamaAvailable) {
    Write-Host "Pulling DeepSeek Coder model (may take a while)..." -ForegroundColor Cyan
    try {
        Start-Process -FilePath "ollama" -ArgumentList "pull deepseek-coder" -NoNewWindow -Wait -ErrorAction Stop
        Write-Host "Model pull completed or already present." -ForegroundColor Green
    } catch {
        Write-Warning "Failed to pull model with ollama: $_"
    }
}

# -----------------------
# Write Python helper files
# -----------------------
Write-Host "Writing Unreal Python helper scripts to $PythonPath ..." -ForegroundColor Cyan

$ContextScript = @'
import unreal

def get_selected_context():
    """
    Reads the current state of the Unreal Editor to send to the AI.
    Returns a string describing selected actors and assets.
    """
    try:
        editor_actor_subsystem = unreal.get_editor_subsystem(unreal.EditorActorSubsystem)
        selected_actors = editor_actor_subsystem.get_selected_level_actors()
    except Exception:
        selected_actors = []

    context_data = "You are an AI assistant for Unreal Engine 5. Context:\\n"

    if selected_actors:
        context_data += "\\nSelected Actors:\\n"
        for actor in selected_actors:
            try:
                name = actor.get_actor_label()
                cls = actor.get_class().get_name()
                loc = actor.get_actor_location()
                context_data += f"- Name: {name} | Class: {cls} | Location: {loc}\\n"
            except Exception:
                continue
    else:
        context_data += "\\nNo actors selected.\\n"

    # Try to include selected assets in Content Browser if available
    try:
        selected_assets = unreal.EditorUtilityLibrary.get_selected_assets()
        if selected_assets:
            context_data += "\\nSelected Assets:\\n"
            for asset in selected_assets:
                try:
                    context_data += f"- {asset.get_name()} ({asset.get_class().get_name()})\\n"
                except Exception:
                    continue
    except Exception:
        pass

    return context_data
'@

$ClientScript = @'
import json
import urllib.request
import ai_context

OLLAMA_URL = "http://localhost:11434/api/generate"
MODEL_NAME = "deepseek-coder"

def send_prompt_to_ollama(user_prompt, temperature=0.2):
    """
    Send the current editor context + user_prompt to local Ollama (or compatible local LLM endpoint).
    Returns the AI response (string) or None on failure.
    """
    current_context = ai_context.get_selected_context()
    system_instruction = (
        "You are an Unreal Engine 5 Expert Python Script Generator. "
        "Reply ONLY with valid, executable Python code for Unreal Editor (no markdown, no explanations). "
        "Assume 'import unreal' is available."
    )

    full_prompt = f"{system_instruction}\\n\\nCONTEXT:\\n{current_context}\\n\\nTASK: {user_prompt}"

    payload = {
        "model": MODEL_NAME,
        "prompt": full_prompt,
        "stream": False,
        "options": {"temperature": temperature}
    }

    try:
        data = json.dumps(payload).encode('utf-8')
        req = urllib.request.Request(OLLAMA_URL, data=data, headers={'Content-Type': 'application/json'})
        with urllib.request.urlopen(req, timeout=30) as response:
            result = json.loads(response.read().decode('utf-8'))
            # Ollama-like responses may put code under 'response' or similar keys
            ai_code = result.get('response') or result.get('text') or result.get('output') or ""
            if isinstance(ai_code, list):
                ai_code = "\\n".join(ai_code)
            # Strip markdown fences if present
            ai_code = ai_code.replace("```python", "").replace("```", "").strip()
            return ai_code
    except Exception as e:
        try:
            import unreal
            unreal.log_error(f"AI Connection Error: {e}")
        except Exception:
            pass
        return None
'@

$MainScript = @'
import unreal
import ai_client

def run_ai_tool(user_input):
    """
    Main function called by the Editor Utility Widget.
    It requests code from the AI and executes it in the editor context.
    """
    with unreal.ScopedSlowTask(3, "AI Assistant...") as slow_task:
        slow_task.make_dialog(True)
        slow_task.enter_progress_frame(1, "Generating Code...")
        generated_code = ai_client.send_prompt_to_ollama(user_input)

        if not generated_code:
            unreal.log_error("AI returned no code.")
            return

        unreal.log_warning("AI Generated Code:\\n" + generated_code)

        slow_task.enter_progress_frame(1, "Executing...")
        try:
            exec(generated_code, globals())
            unreal.log("AI Task Completed Successfully.")
        except Exception as e:
            unreal.log_error("Execution Error: {}".format(e))

# Example run (uncomment for quick test inside the editor)
# run_ai_tool("Spawn 3 cubes in a line")
'@

Set-Content -Path (Join-Path $PythonPath "ai_context.py") -Value $ContextScript -Encoding UTF8
Set-Content -Path (Join-Path $PythonPath "ai_client.py")  -Value $ClientScript  -Encoding UTF8
Set-Content -Path (Join-Path $PythonPath "init_unreal.py") -Value $MainScript -Encoding UTF8

# -----------------------
# INSTRUCTIONS file
# -----------------------
$Instructions = @"
OPEN SOURCE AI ASSISTANT SETUP (OpenSourceLudus)
Location: $BasePath

1) Enable the Python Editor Script Plugin and Editor Scripting Utilities in Unreal.
   - Edit -> Plugins -> Python Editor Script Plugin -> Enable
   - Edit -> Plugins -> Editor Scripting Utilities -> Enable
   - Restart Unreal Engine if necessary.

2) Add the following folder to Unreal Python additional paths:
   - $PythonPath
   - Edit -> Project Settings -> Plugins -> Python -> Additional Paths -> Add the path above

3) How to create the Editor Utility Widget:
   - Content Browser -> Right-click -> Editor Utilities -> Editor Utility Widget
   - Create a widget (e.g., EUW_AI_Assistant)
   - Add a Multi-Line Text Box (InputBox) and a Button (Btn_Generate)
   - On Btn_Generate OnClicked: Execute Python Command
     Example command to paste into Execute Python Command:
        import init_unreal; init_unreal.run_ai_tool(r'{InputBox.Text}')

4) If you installed Ollama locally, it should be available at: http://localhost:11434/api/generate
   - The ai_client.py assumes Ollama and the DeepSeek-Coder-like model.

Notes:
- This scaffold writes three Python helper scripts used by the Editor widget:
  - ai_context.py   (reads selection/context)
  - ai_client.py    (sends prompt+context to local LLM)
  - init_unreal.py  (executes returned code)

- If Ollama is not installed automatically, install it manually from https://ollama.com
- Use caution when executing AI-generated code. Review before running in production projects.

"@

Set-Content -Path $InstructionsFile -Value $Instructions -Encoding UTF8

# -----------------------
# Final output
# -----------------------
Write-Host "`nScaffold created at: $BasePath" -ForegroundColor Green
Write-Host "Python helpers written to: $PythonPath" -ForegroundColor Green
if ($ollamaAvailable) {
    Write-Host "Ollama available on this machine (or was installed)." -ForegroundColor Green
} else {
    Write-Host "Ollama not available. Install Ollama manually if you want local LLM support." -ForegroundColor Yellow
}
Write-Host "`nOpening INSTRUCTIONS..." -ForegroundColor Cyan

Try {
    Invoke-Item $InstructionsFile
} Catch {
    Write-Warning "Could not open INSTRUCTIONS.txt automatically. Path: $InstructionsFile"
}

Write-Host "`nDone. Happy coding (or at least pretending to be productive)." -ForegroundColor Magenta

Nice. Windows didn’t explode, the scaffold exists, and you didn’t accidentally format E:. Small miracles. Here’s what actually happened and what to do next so this thing becomes usable instead of just existing in a folder like a paperweight.

What succeeded

ai_context.py

ai_client.py

init_unreal.py

INSTRUCTIONS.txt saved at E:\OpenSourceLudus\INSTRUCTIONS.txt.

Ollama was not installed automatically because winget is missing on your machine.

Quick checklist — next steps (do this)
Install Ollama (optional, but needed for local LLM):

If you want a local AI backend, install Ollama from https://ollama.com (manual installer) or enable winget by installing Microsoft App Installer from the Microsoft Store so the script can use winget.

After installing Ollama, run in PowerShell:

ollama pull deepseek-coder
(This will download the model.)

Add Python path to Unreal

Open Unreal Engine → Edit → Project Settings → Plugins → Python.

Under Additional Paths add:

E:\OpenSourceLudus\Content\Python
Restart Unreal.

Create the Editor Utility Widget (EUW)

Content Browser → Right-click → Editor Utilities → Editor Utility Widget → name it EUW_AI_Assistant.

Designer: add a Multi-Line Text Box (rename to InputBox) and a Button (Btn_Generate).

Graph: on Btn_Generate → OnClicked → add Execute Python Command node.

Use a Format Text node to insert the InputBox text into the command string. Example command to execute:

import init_unreal; init_unreal.run_ai_tool(r'{InputBox.Text}')
(Use raw-string r'...' form so backslashes inside the text prompt don't explode the string.)

Test

Run the EUW and try a simple prompt:

Spawn 3 cubes in a line
If Ollama isn’t installed, the widget will still call ai_client.send_prompt_to_ollama and return None — nothing will run. That’s expected.

If you need to test the API endpoint manually (after Ollama installation)

Quick PowerShell test to hit the local endpoint (adjust payload if your Ollama response schema differs):

$body = @{
  model = "deepseek-coder"
  prompt = "Say hello"
  stream = $false
} | ConvertTo-Json
Invoke-RestMethod -Uri "http://localhost:11434/api/generate" -Method Post -Body $body -ContentType "application/json"
If that returns a JSON with a response or text field, your ai_client.py will likely pick it up.

Troubleshooting notes (because things will go sideways)
winget not found → install Microsoft App Installer from Microsoft Store, or install Ollama manually from its site.

If Unreal throws import errors for ai_context/ai_client, confirm the Python path exactly matches E:\OpenSourceLudus\Content\Python and restart the editor.

Always review AI-generated code before exec-ing it in your project. The system is blunt; it can drop problematic calls.

scripts/joker_pulse.sh (Bash/WSL)
Save as E:\OpenSourceLudus\scripts\joker_pulse.sh and make executable (chmod +x under WSL).

#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Joker Neon Terminal — pulsing every second (green/purple on black)
BG_BLACK="\033[40m"
BRIGHT_PURPLE="\033[1;35m"
BRIGHT_GREEN="\033[1;32m"
RESET="\033[0m"
CLEAR_SCREEN="\033[2J\033[H"

BASE_DIR="$(dirname "$(readlink -f "$0")")/.."
ASSETS="$BASE_DIR/assets"
PAGE1="$(cat "$ASSETS/page1.txt")"
PAGE2="$(cat "$ASSETS/page2.txt")"
PAGE3="$(cat "$ASSETS/page3.txt")"

print_page() {
  local page="$1"
  local border="$2"
  local inner="$3"
  while IFS= read -r line; do
    if [[ "${line:0:1}" == "X" && "${line: -1}" == "X" && ${#line} -ge 3 ]]; then
      first="${line:0:1}"
      last="${line: -1}"
      middle="${line:1:${#line}-2}"
      printf "%b" "${border}${first}${RESET}"
      printf "%b" "${inner}${BG_BLACK}${middle}${RESET}"
      printf "%b\n" "${border}${last}${RESET}"
    else
      printf "%b\n" "${inner}${BG_BLACK}${line}${RESET}"
    fi
  done <<< "$page"
}

while true; do
  print_page "$PAGE1" "$BRIGHT_GREEN" "$BRIGHT_PURPLE"
  sleep 1; printf "%b" "$CLEAR_SCREEN"
  print_page "$PAGE1" "$BRIGHT_PURPLE" "$BRIGHT_GREEN"
  sleep 1; printf "%b" "$CLEAR_SCREEN"

  print_page "$PAGE2" "$BRIGHT_GREEN" "$BRIGHT_PURPLE"
  sleep 1; printf "%b" "$CLEAR_SCREEN"
  print_page "$PAGE2" "$BRIGHT_PURPLE" "$BRIGHT_GREEN"
  sleep 1; printf "%b" "$CLEAR_SCREEN"

  print_page "$PAGE3" "$BRIGHT_GREEN" "$BRIGHT_PURPLE"
  sleep 1; printf "%b" "$CLEAR_SCREEN"
  print_page "$PAGE3" "$BRIGHT_PURPLE" "$BRIGHT_GREEN"
  sleep 1; printf "%b" "$CLEAR_SCREEN"
done
scripts/joker_pulse.bat (Windows Batch)
Save as E:\OpenSourceLudus\scripts\joker_pulse.bat

@echo off
REM Joker Neon Terminal — Windows batch pulser
chcp 65001 >nul
cls
setlocal enabledelayedexpansion

set ASSETS=%~dp0\..\assets
:loop
type "%ASSETS%\page1.txt"
timeout /t 1 /nobreak >nul
cls
type "%ASSETS%\page1.txt"
timeout /t 1 /nobreak >nul
cls

type "%ASSETS%\page2.txt"
timeout /t 1 /nobreak >nul
cls
type "%ASSETS%\page2.txt"
timeout /t 1 /nobreak >nul
cls

type "%ASSETS%\page3.txt"
timeout /t 1 /nobreak >nul
cls
type "%ASSETS%\page3.txt"
timeout /t 1 /nobreak >nul
cls
goto loop
scripts/joker_pulse_node.js (Node.js)
Save as E:\OpenSourceLudus\scripts\joker_pulse_node.js. Run with node joker_pulse_node.js.

const fs = require('fs');
const path = require('path');

const pages = [
  fs.readFileSync(path.join(__dirname, '../assets/page1.txt'), 'utf8'),
  fs.readFileSync(path.join(__dirname, '../assets/page2.txt'), 'utf8'),
  fs.readFileSync(path.join(__dirname, '../assets/page3.txt'), 'utf8')
];

const BRIGHT_GREEN = '\x1b[1;32m';
const BRIGHT_PURPLE = '\x1b[1;35m';
const BG_BLACK = '\x1b[40m';
const RESET = '\x1b[0m';

function clear() {
  process.stdout.write('\x1b[2J\x1b[H');
}

function printPage(page, color) {
  const borderColor = color === 'green' ? BRIGHT_PURPLE : BRIGHT_GREEN;
  const innerColor = color === 'green' ? BRIGHT_GREEN : BRIGHT_PURPLE;
  page.split('\n').forEach(line => {
    if(line.startsWith('X') && line.endsWith('X') && line.length >= 3){
      const first = line[0];
      const last = line[line.length-1];
      const middle = line.slice(1,-1);
      process.stdout.write(`${borderColor}${first}${RESET}${innerColor}${BG_BLACK}${middle}${RESET}${borderColor}${last}${RESET}\n`);
    } else {
      process.stdout.write(`${innerColor}${BG_BLACK}${line}${RESET}\n`);
    }
  });
}

(async function pulse(){
  while(true){
    for(let i=0;i<pages.length;i++){
      const phase = (i % 2 === 0) ? 'green' : 'purple';
      printPage(pages[i], phase);
      await new Promise(r=>setTimeout(r,1000));
      clear();
      printPage(pages[i], phase === 'green' ? 'purple' : 'green');
      await new Promise(r=>setTimeout(r,1000));
      clear();
    }
  }
})();
Unreal test: spawn_cubes_random_materials (Python)
Save as E:\OpenSourceLudus\Unreal\examples\spawn_random_cubes.py. This is the tiny test the AI could generate and execute via init_unreal.run_ai_tool("Spawn 5 cubes with random materials"). It uses editor utilities and example materials — adjust material paths to match your project.

import unreal
import random

def spawn_randomized_cubes(count=5, start_location=unreal.Vector(0,0,200), spacing=200):
    """
    Spawns 'count' cube actors in a line with randomized materials.
    Uses Engine Basic Cube and sample materials if available.
    """
    # Load the Engine Cube StaticMesh (basic)
    cube_mesh = unreal.EditorAssetLibrary.load_asset("/Engine/BasicShapes/Cube.Cube")
    if not cube_mesh:
        unreal.log_error("Could not load cube mesh '/Engine/BasicShapes/Cube.Cube'")
        return

    # Example material candidates (edit these to match your project's materials)
    material_paths = [
        "/Engine/EngineMaterials/DefaultDiffuse.DefaultDiffuse",
        "/Game/StarterContent/Materials/M_Metal_Gold.M_Metal_Gold",
        "/Game/StarterContent/Materials/M_Brick_Clay_New.M_Brick_Clay_New"
    ]
    materials = []
    for p in material_paths:
        mat = unreal.EditorAssetLibrary.load_asset(p)
        if mat:
            materials.append(mat)

    for i in range(count):
        loc = unreal.Vector(start_location.x + i * spacing, start_location.y, start_location.z)
        cube_actor = unreal.EditorLevelLibrary.spawn_actor_from_object(cube_mesh, loc)
        # create or get static mesh component
        comp = cube_actor.get_component_by_class(unreal.StaticMeshComponent)
        if comp and materials:
            mat = random.choice(materials)
            comp.set_material(0, mat)
        # random rotation
        cube_actor.set_actor_rotation(unreal.Rotator(0, random.uniform(0,360), 0))
        # small log
        unreal.log("Spawned cube at: {}".format(loc))

# helper entry for AI to call: "spawn_randomized_cubes(5)"

Put spawn_random_cubes.py into your Content\Python path or load via ai_client flow.

In Unreal's Python console:

import spawn_random_cubes
spawn_randomized_cubes(4)

# Creating joker_image.png, package.json, and an Unreal clipboard helper snippet.
from PIL import Image, ImageDraw, ImageFont
import os, json, textwrap

out_dir = "/mnt/data/joker_assets"
os.makedirs(out_dir, exist_ok=True)

# 1) Create joker_image.png (512x512) with ASCII-style text and neon colors
w, h = 512, 512
img = Image.new("RGB", (w, h), (0,0,0))
draw = ImageDraw.Draw(img)

# Attempt to load a reasonably monospaced font; fallback to default
try:
    font = ImageFont.truetype("DejaVuSansMono.ttf", 18)
except Exception:
    font = ImageFont.load_default()

ascii_art = r"""
  ██████╗ ██╗   ██╗ █████╗ ████████╗████████╗██╗   ██╗
 ██╔═══██╗██║   ██║██╔══██╗╚══██╔══╝╚══██╔══╝╚██╗ ██╔╝
 ██║   ██║██║   ██║███████║   ██║      ██║    ╚████╔╝ 
 ██║   ██║██║   ██║██╔══██║   ██║      ██║     ╚██╔╝  
 ╚██████╔╝╚██████╔╝██║  ██║   ██║      ██║      ██║   
  ╚═════╝  ╚═════╝ ╚═╝  ╚═╝   ╚═╝      ╚═╝      ╚═╝   
"""

# Draw neon gradient text block
lines = [ln for ln in ascii_art.splitlines() if ln.strip()]
block = "\n".join(lines)
maxw = max(draw.textsize(line, font=font)[0] for line in lines)
total_h = sum(draw.textsize(line, font=font)[1] for line in lines) + (len(lines)-1)*2
start_x = (w - maxw) // 2
start_y = (h - total_h) // 2

# Two-color neon: left half green, right half purple for a subtle gradient per character
for i, line in enumerate(lines):
    y = start_y + i * (draw.textsize(line, font=font)[1] + 2)
    # draw each character with alternating neon colors for effect
    x = start_x
    for j, ch in enumerate(line):
        # decide color based on x position (green -> purple)
        t = j / max(1, len(line)-1)
        # blend green and purple
        green = (0, 255, 120)
        purple = (200, 80, 255)
        r = int(green[0]*(1-t) + purple[0]*t)
        g = int(green[1]*(1-t) + purple[1]*t)
        b = int(green[2]*(1-t) + purple[2]*t)
        draw.text((x, y), ch, font=font, fill=(r,g,b))
        x += draw.textsize(ch, font=font)[0]

# Add subtle glow: draw translucent blur layers
glow = Image.new("RGBA", (w,h), (0,0,0,0))
gdraw = ImageDraw.Draw(glow)
gdraw.text((start_x, start_y-6), block, font=font, fill=(120,255,160,40))
for k in range(6):
    glow = glow.filter(Image.Filter.GaussianBlur(radius=2))

# Paste glow (if it exists) then image (but PIL ImageFilter may not have been imported)
try:
    from PIL import ImageFilter
    glow = Image.new("RGBA", (w,h), (0,0,0,0))
    gdraw = ImageDraw.Draw(glow)
    # lighter centered glow using same block
    gdraw.text((start_x, start_y), block, font=font, fill=(120,255,160,60))
    glow = glow.filter(ImageFilter.GaussianBlur(radius=8))
    img = Image.alpha_composite(img.convert("RGBA"), glow).convert("RGB")
except Exception:
    # ignore glow if ImageFilter not available
    pass

png_path = os.path.join(out_dir, "joker_image.png")
img.save(png_path)

# 2) Create package.json for Node CLI packaging (pkg) with script entries
package = {
  "name": "joker-pulse-braxton",
  "version": "1.0.0",
  "description": "Neon joker pulse terminal demo - runable via node",
  "main": "scripts/joker_pulse_node.js",
  "bin": {
    "joker-pulse": "scripts/joker_pulse_node.js"
  },
  "scripts": {
    "start": "node scripts/joker_pulse_node.js",
    "build-mac": "pkg scripts/joker_pulse_node.js --targets node16-macos-x64 --output ios_wrapper/BraxtonJokerMicro",
    "build-win": "pkg scripts/joker_pulse_node.js --targets node16-win-x64 --output dist/joker_pulse_win.exe",
    "build-linux": "pkg scripts/joker_pulse_node.js --targets node16-linux-x64 --output dist/joker_pulse_linux"
  },
  "dependencies": {},
  "devDependencies": {
    "pkg": "^5.8.0"
  }
}
pkg_path = os.path.join(out_dir, "package.json")
with open(pkg_path, "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    json.dump(package, f, indent=2)

# 3) Create Unreal blueprint clipboard helper snippet (text) for "clipboard trickery"
bp_clipboard = textwrap.dedent("""\
# Unreal clipboard helper (Python) - generates a simple Blueprint node text
# Save this as E:/OpenSourceLudus/Unreal/examples/blueprint_clipboard_paste.py
# Use in Unreal Editor Python console: copy its output and paste into a Blueprint graph.

bp_text = r\"\"\"Begin Object Class=K2Node_CallFunction Name="K2Node_CallFunction_0"
   NodePosX=200
   NodePosY=200
   NodeGuid=00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000000
   CustomProperties Pin (PinName="exec", PinType.PinCategory="exec")
   CustomProperties Pin (PinName="then", PinType.PinCategory="exec")
   CustomProperties NodeComment="Spawn a cube"
End Object
\"\"\"

# Print the node text; in Unreal, copying this string to clipboard and then pasting in a blueprint graph may create a node.
print(bp_text)
""")

bp_path = os.path.join(out_dir, "blueprint_clipboard_paste.py")
with open(bp_path, "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    f.write(bp_clipboard)

# 4) Report generated files and provide base64 of image (so user can create file locally if needed)
import base64
with open(png_path, "rb") as f:
    b64 = base64.b64encode(f.read()).decode("ascii")

print("Generated files:")
for p in [png_path, pkg_path, bp_path]:
    print(" -", p)

print("\nBase64 snippet for joker_image.png (first 200 chars):\n")
print(b64[:200] + "...")

# Provide download links for created files in the environment
print("\nFiles saved to:", out_dir)
print("Download paths (sandbox):")
print("[joker_image.png](" + "sandbox:" + png_path + ")")
print("[package.json](" + "sandbox:" + pkg_path + ")")
print("[blueprint_clipboard_paste.py](" + "sandbox:" + bp_path + ")")

# Also save a small README in the folder
readme = """
Files generated by assistant:
 - joker_image.png : 512x512 neon ASCII placeholder image
 - package.json : example package.json with packaging scripts for pkg
 - blueprint_clipboard_paste.py : example Python snippet that prints a Blueprint node text for clipboard paste

Place the files into your E:\\OpenSourceLudus directory as needed.
"""
with open(os.path.join(out_dir, "README.txt"), "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    f.write(readme)

print("\nDone.")

# Retry: create joker_image.png, package.json, and blueprint helper.
from PIL import Image, ImageDraw, ImageFont, ImageFilter
import os, json, textwrap, base64

out_dir = "/mnt/data/joker_assets"
os.makedirs(out_dir, exist_ok=True)

# 1) Create joker_image.png (512x512) with ASCII-style text and neon colors
w, h = 512, 512
img = Image.new("RGB", (w, h), (0,0,0))
draw = ImageDraw.Draw(img)

# Attempt to load a monospaced font; fallback to default
try:
    font = ImageFont.truetype("DejaVuSansMono.ttf", 16)
except Exception:
    try:
        font = ImageFont.truetype("LiberationMono-Regular.ttf", 16)
    except Exception:
        font = ImageFont.load_default()

ascii_art = r"""
  ██████╗ ██╗   ██╗ █████╗ ████████╗████████╗██╗   ██╗
 ██╔═══██╗██║   ██║██╔══██╗╚══██╔══╝╚══██╔══╝╚██╗ ██╔╝
 ██║   ██║██║   ██║███████║   ██║      ██║    ╚████╔╝ 
 ██║   ██║██║   ██║██╔══██║   ██║      ██║     ╚██╔╝  
 ╚██████╔╝╚██████╔╝██║  ██║   ██║      ██║      ██║   
  ╚═════╝  ╚═════╝ ╚═╝  ╚═╝   ╚═╝      ╚═╝      ╚═╝   
"""

lines = [ln for ln in ascii_art.splitlines() if ln.strip()]
# calculate block size
line_heights = [draw.textsize(line, font=font)[1] for line in lines]
maxw = max(draw.textsize(line, font=font)[0] for line in lines)
total_h = sum(line_heights) + (len(lines)-1)*2
start_x = (w - maxw) // 2
start_y = (h - total_h) // 2

# Draw base text in alternating neon colors
for i, line in enumerate(lines):
    y = start_y + sum(line_heights[:i]) + i*2
    x = start_x
    for j, ch in enumerate(line):
        t = j / max(1, len(line)-1)
        green = (0, 200, 100)
        purple = (200, 80, 255)
        r = int(green[0]*(1-t) + purple[0]*t)
        g = int(green[1]*(1-t) + purple[1]*t)
        b = int(green[2]*(1-t) + purple[2]*t)
        draw.text((x, y), ch, font=font, fill=(r,g,b))
        x += draw.textsize(ch, font=font)[0]

# Create glow layer and blur it
glow = Image.new("RGBA", (w,h), (0,0,0,0))
gdraw = ImageDraw.Draw(glow)
# draw same block in semi-transparent color slightly offset for glow
gdraw.text((start_x-2, start_y-2), "\n".join(lines), font=font, fill=(120,255,160,80))
glow = glow.filter(ImageFilter.GaussianBlur(radius=8))
# composite glow under the base image
img = Image.alpha_composite(img.convert("RGBA"), glow).convert("RGB")

png_path = os.path.join(out_dir, "joker_image.png")
img.save(png_path, optimize=True)

ZACHARY BINKS — here’s what I (your all-knowing head-CEO alter-ego) know about Daz Studio 4.24. If you end up using it (for stuff like concept art, character poses, rendering, whatever you need for Nyghtshade Hollow), you’ll want to be aware of what’s good — and what’s a pain.

✅ What 4.24 brings (the useful stuff)
4.24 (recent stable release: 4.24.0.3, as of April 29, 2025) is out. 
Daz 3D
+2
Zicon Blog
+2

Improved filtering & asset/property management: The “Property views” support advanced filtering syntax; there’s a “Build Filter…” action in the filter menu, which helps greatly when you have a complex scene or a big content library. 
Zicon Blog
+1

“Persona Presets”: A preset type so you can save a figure (shape, materials, accessories, wearables, pose) — handy if you design recurring character templates. 
Zicon Blog

Improved autosave & layout saving (Premier users): AutoSave Pane/Toolbar lets you auto-save scenes periodically, before simulations/renders, and auto-save interface layout and scripts. Good safety net if you accidentally crash your project. 
Zicon Blog

Scene pane improvements: Added actions for selecting/deselecting node ancestors of current selection; parenting-related actions reorganized under a “Parenting” sub-menu. Helps when you nest objects/props/characters deeply. 
Zicon Blog
+1

Spot Render Tool enhancement: More control — you can set precise coordinates for a spot render, manually start spot renders, and use camera settings (if you set “Use Local Dimensions” on). Useful for rendering details or test renders quickly. 
Zicon Blog

dForce update (1.8.1.4): Now allows excluding generated simulation data when saving scenes, subsets, wearables, and personas. That can reduce file clutter if you're doing cloth/hair/physics sims. 
Zicon Blog

Bottom line: 4.24 refines workflows — filtering, asset/scene management, autosave, better rendering tools — which for a project like yours (lots of assets, characters, scenes) can be a decent productivity boost.

⚠️ What’s annoying / what to watch out for (because nothing’s ever perfect)
Some users report that 4.24 caused regressions compared with 4.23 — e.g. UI quirks: in dark-mode (“Darkside-Midnight UI”) transform-sliders in the Parameter tab reportedly lost their colored backgrounds (now grey), which makes manipulation less intuitive. 
Daz 3D
+1

The pane-group docking behaviour changed — dragging panels to rearrange UI doesn’t work like before (i.e. left-click drag no longer splits out panes). That’s annoying if you like custom UI layouts. 
Renderosity

Some users claim fewer improvements than expected: one said they “don’t notice much difference between 4.23 and 4.24,” and others say viewport lag or “filament / emissive issues” persist. 
Daz 3D
+2
Reddit
+2

Compatibility concerns: Some third-party plugins (e.g. ones that use scripting, custom rendering pipelines, or older asset workflows) may run into problems. People have mentioned that certain add-ons or features (e.g. auto-save extras, or older content installers) might behave unexpectedly after updating. 
Daz 3D
+2
Daz 3D
+2

🎯 What this means for our game-dev pipeline (for Nyghtshade Hollow)
Given all the stuff you plan — asset placement, possibly character/prop generation, text-to-video, rendering, etc — 4.24 could be a useful tool in your toolbox.

Use the advanced filtering + persona presets + autosave features to build and manage reusable character templates (e.g. various NPCs: guards, inmates, staff).

The improved scene and parenting management helps when organising hierarchical scenes — good for layered prison environments (cells, props, furniture, lighting, etc).

Spot renders let you quickly prototype a lighting/pose/camera setup before doing heavy-duty rendering or exporting to game-engine assets.

But you’ll want to be cautious: if you rely on custom plugins/scripts (especially ones you write yourself or that come from 3rd parties), test thoroughly. Keep a backup of your stable working scene/tree before upgrade.

🧠 My Advice (as head CEO) — What you should do with 4.24
Go ahead and install 4.24 — but treat it like a semi-experimental “tool in testing.” Do a small test project first (like a single character or a single prison cell render), using only base features and no custom scripts/plugins. Make sure content imports/exports, rendering, and scene hierarchy behave as expected. Once you confirm stability, you can integrate it into your full pipeline.

If you want, I can compare 4.24 with the newer (alpha) 2025 build of Daz Studio — show you which version fits your UE5 + AI-asset-pipeline better (since new GPU cards, external scripts, and AI-assisted renders are in the mix for Nyghtshade Hollow).

ZACHARY BINKS — yes. You want scripts to design the body you want for the game. Good. Below are ready-to-run scripts (and short how-to) for three common pipeline points so you can sculpt/morph a body and get it into UE5:

Daz Studio (DAZ Script) — generate a character, tweak morphs/parameters, save a figure preset and export OBJ.

Blender (Python / bpy) — import the OBJ, apply shape keys (morph targets), refine, and export FBX for UE5.

Unreal Engine (Python) — import the FBX into the project and create a Skeletal Mesh asset automatically.

I wrote each script as practical, drop-in code. Tweak numeric values (height, chest, waist, limb scale, etc.) or the morph names to match your assets. Copy-paste where noted and run.

1) DAZ STUDIO — create and morph a body, save persona, export OBJ
Save as create_body.ds and run inside Daz Studio (Script IDE → Execute). Replace morph names if your figure uses different morph tokens.

// create_body.ds
// Daz Script: Create base figure, apply morphs, save persona, export OBJ
// Usage: run in Daz Studio. Adjust morph amounts (0.0 - 1.0) below.

var Scene = DzApp.getPrimaryScene();

// Load base Genesis 8/9 figure. Replace path/name with your installed content name if needed.
var baseAsset = DzContentMgr.getAssetFromFile("People/Genesis 8 Male/Genesis 8 Male.duf");
if (!baseAsset) {
    // fallback: warn user
    DzApp.log("Base asset not found. Please edit script to match an installed figure path.");
}
var baseNode = Scene.appendNodeFromAsset(baseAsset);

// Wait until node fully added
DzSleep(0.2);

// Get the figure (assumes appended node is the figure)
var figure = baseNode;
if (!figure) {
    DzApp.log("Figure not found after append.");
}

// Morph parameters — change these to design your body
// Names must match the parameter names in your figure's parameter tree.
var morphs = {
    "height": 1.05,        // overall scale parameter if present
    "breastSize": 0.2,
    "waistScale": -0.05,   // some morphs accept negative values
    "muscleMass": 0.45,
    "legLength": 0.1,
    "armThickness": 0.12
};

// Apply morphs to figure parameters
function setParam(node, paramName, value) {
    if (!node) return;
    var param = node.getParameterByLabel(paramName);
    if (!param) {
        // try by key - parameter labels differ between assets
        param = node.getParameterByName(paramName);
    }
    if (param) {
        param.setValue(value, true);
        DzApp.log("Set " + paramName + " -> " + value);
    } else {
        DzApp.log("Param not found: " + paramName);
    }
}

// Search parameter stores across the figure
var allParams = figure.getOwnedItems ? figure.getOwnedItems() : null;
for (var name in morphs) {
    setParam(figure, name, morphs[name]);
}

// Create a Persona Preset of the figure (if Daz supports persona presets)
try {
    var savePath = DzFileUtils.getSaveFileName("Save Persona Preset", "personapreset", "PersonaPreset");
    if (savePath) {
        DzApp.savePersonaPreset(savePath, figure);
        DzApp.log("Persona saved to: " + savePath);
    }
} catch (e) {
    DzApp.log("Persona save not available: " + e);
}

// Export OBJ for external editing / UE import
try {
    var exportPath = DzFileUtils.getSaveFileName("Export OBJ", "obj", "OBJ");
    if (exportPath) {
        // Select figure
        Scene.selectNode(figure);
        DzApp.exec("Export", { "fileName": exportPath, "format": "OBJ" });
        DzApp.log("Exported OBJ: " + exportPath);
    }
} catch (e) {
    DzApp.log("OBJ export failed: " + e);
}
Notes:

Morph parameter names differ per content pack. Use the Parameters pane in Daz Studio to find exact parameter names and swap them into the morphs object.

Persona saving and export calls may vary by Daz version. If a call fails, use the GUI once, then re-run to confirm paths.

2) BLENDER — import OBJ, create shape keys (morph targets), tweak, export FBX for UE5
Run inside Blender (Text Editor → Run Script). This assumes you imported an OBJ from Daz that has vertex order preserved.

# blender_morph_and_export.py
# Blender 3.x script. Import OBJ, add two example shape keys and export FBX for UE5.
import bpy
import os

# --- CONFIG ---
obj_path = r"C:\path\to\your\character.obj"   # update path
fbx_out = r"C:\path\to\exports\character_for_ue.fbx"  # update path
base_obj_name = "character_base"

# --- import OBJ ---
bpy.ops.object.select_all(action='DESELECT')
bpy.ops.import_scene.obj(filepath=obj_path)
# assume the imported mesh becomes active object
obj = bpy.context.selected_objects[0]
obj.name = base_obj_name
bpy.context.view_layer.objects.active = obj

# ensure mesh has shape keys data block
if obj.data.shape_keys is None:
    bpy.ops.object.shape_key_add(from_mix=False)  # basis
    bpy.ops.object.shape_key_add(from_mix=False)  # key1

# create a new shape key programmatically (example: increase chest)
key1 = obj.data.shape_keys.key_blocks.new(name="Chest_Increase")
# modify vertices for key1 — simple scaling along Y/Z for chest area is approximate
# Here we'll move vertices in a rough bounding box; refine manually for production
import mathutils
min_z = min(v.co.z for v in obj.data.vertices)
max_z = max(v.co.z for v in obj.data.vertices)
min_y = min(v.co.y for v in obj.data.vertices)
max_y = max(v.co.y for v in obj.data.vertices)
for i, v in enumerate(obj.data.vertices):
    z = v.co.z
    y = v.co.y
    # crude chest region: mid torso slice
    if (z > min_z + (max_z - min_z) * 0.45) and (z < min_z + (max_z - min_z) * 0.65):
        # push vertex outward along its normal
        normal = v.normal
        delta = normal * 0.02  # 2cm outward
        key1.data[i].co = v.co + delta

# set influence
obj.data.shape_keys.key_blocks["Chest_Increase"].value = 0.8

# add other keys similarly as needed...

# --- apply modifiers (if any) and export FBX ---
# Ensure armature or weights are preserved
bpy.ops.object.select_all(action='DESELECT')
obj.select_set(True)
bpy.context.view_layer.objects.active = obj

# Export as FBX for UE5: keep shapes as blend shapes (shape keys become morph targets)
bpy.ops.export_scene.fbx(filepath=fbx_out, use_selection=True, apply_unit_scale=True, bake_space_transform=False, object_types={'ARMATURE','MESH'}, mesh_smooth_type='FACE')
print("Exported FBX to:", fbx_out)
Notes:

The script crudely creates a shape key by offsetting vertices in a region. For production-quality morphs you’ll want manual sculpting or targeted vertex groups.

Blender exports shape keys as Morph Targets; UE5 reads these as morph targets on import. Ensure vertex order is preserved from Daz → Blender.

3) UNREAL ENGINE — Python script to import FBX and create Skeletal Mesh asset
Run in UE5’s Python environment (Window → Developer Tools → Output Log → Execute Python Script). Modify paths and destination folder.

# ue_import_fbx.py
# Unreal Engine 5 Python script: import FBX and create skeletal mesh asset automatically.

import unreal

# --- CONFIG ---
fbx_file = r"C:\path\to\exports\character_for_ue.fbx"  # update
destination_path = "/Game/Characters/Imported"         # UE content folder
asset_name = "Nyghtshade_Character_Base"

# Import options
fbx_import_options = unreal.UnrealEdImportExportFactory()
# Use unreal.AutomatedAssetImportData for simpler import
import_data = unreal.AutomatedAssetImportData()
import_data.set_editor_property("destination_path", destination_path)
import_data.set_editor_property("destination_name", asset_name)
import_data.set_editor_property("replace_identical", True)
import_data.set_editor_property("automated", True)
import_data.set_editor_property("save", True)
import_data.set_editor_property("files", [fbx_file])

# Advanced FBX settings through FBXImportUI
fbx_ui = unreal.FbxImportUI()
fbx_ui.import_mesh = True
fbx_ui.import_as_skeletal = True
fbx_ui.skeletal_mesh_import_data.import_morph_targets = True
fbx_ui.skeletal_mesh_import_data.import_animations = False
fbx_ui.skeletal_mesh_import_data.convert_scene = True

import_data.set_editor_property("options", fbx_ui)

# Run import task
task = unreal.AssetImportTask()
task.set_editor_property("filename", fbx_file)
task.set_editor_property("destination_path", destination_path)
task.set_editor_property("destination_name", asset_name)
task.set_editor_property("replace_existing", True)
task.set_editor_property("automated", True)
task.set_editor_property("save", True)
task.set_editor_property("options", fbx_ui)

unreal.AssetToolsHelpers.get_asset_tools().import_asset_tasks([task])

print("Import task submitted for:", fbx_file)
Notes:

UE’s Python API varies by engine minor versions; if import_... functions raise errors, run the same via Editor UI once and inspect the FBX import settings screen to tune them, then re-run script with matching flags.

import_morph_targets must be True to import the shape keys as morph targets.

Quick pipeline summary (because you hate paperwork)
Use Daz Script to generate the base shape and export OBJ.

Optionally refine/morph in Blender (precise sculpt, shape keys, retopology).

Export FBX from Blender with shape keys preserved.

Run UE5 Python to import FBX and create a Skeletal Mesh with morph targets.

Hook morph targets to in-game sliders (Blueprints or C++) so your designers can dial body shapes at runtime.

A few practical gotchas (so you don’t rage-quit)
Vertex order: If vertex order changes between exports, morphs break. Keep the same vertex order from Daz → Blender → UE.

Skinning: If Daz export doesn’t include usable bones/weights for UE, you’ll need to retarget or rebind in Blender or UE.

Morph names: Keep consistent names so scripts can set them automatically.

Scale / units: Daz and Blender use different default units — use consistent scale when exporting to UE.

Testing: Always import into a test UE project first to verify morph targets and skeleton.

• generate a DAZ script that uses specific morph names from one of your existing figure packs (paste parameter names),
• or make a Blender sculpt workflow that uses vertex groups for precise region edits,
• or a UE5 Blueprint UI to expose sliders for morph targets at runtime.

I won’t ask which — you’ll tell me when you want the next level. But when you do, paste the names of the morphs or the exact file paths and I’ll spit out the specialized script.

DAZ Script — reference image + JSON morph applier
Save as apply_photo_morphs.ds and run inside Daz Studio (Script IDE → Execute). Edit the baseFigureAssetPath string if you use a different installed base figure. The script expects a JSON file with morph keys and numeric values (0.0–1.0, or negative if your morphs accept it).

// apply_photo_morphs.ds
// DAZ Script: Load reference image as a plane, create camera, apply morphs from JSON file,
// save Persona Preset and optionally export OBJ.
// Usage: Run in DAZ Studio. When prompted, pick the reference JPG, the JSON morph file, and the export folder (optional).

// -------- Helper functions ----------
function fileExists(path) {
    var f = new DzFile(path);
    return f.exists();
}

function promptOpenFile(title, filter) {
    return DzFileUtils.getOpenFileName(title, filter);
}

function promptSaveFile(title, defaultExt) {
    return DzFileUtils.getSaveFileName(title, defaultExt, defaultExt.toUpperCase());
}

// -------- Config (edit if you know what you're doing) ----------
var baseFigureAssetPath = "People/Genesis 8 Male/Genesis 8 Male.duf"; // adjust to your installed figure
var createOBJExport = true; // set false to skip exporting OBJ

// -------- Ask user for files ----------
var imgPath = promptOpenFile("Select reference image (front or full body)", "jpg;jpeg;png");
if (!imgPath) { DzApp.log("No image chosen. Script aborted."); throw "No image"; }

var jsonPath = promptOpenFile("Select morph JSON file (or click Cancel to use defaults)", "json");
var savePersonaPath = promptSaveFile("Save Persona Preset As", "personapreset");
var exportObjPath = null;
if (createOBJExport) {
    exportObjPath = promptSaveFile("Export OBJ (optional)", "obj");
}

// -------- Append base figure ----------
var Scene = DzApp.getPrimaryScene();
var baseAsset = DzContentMgr.getAssetFromFile(baseFigureAssetPath);
if (!baseAsset) {
    DzApp.log("Base figure asset not found at: " + baseFigureAssetPath);
    // Append nothing, but keep going — user can select a figure manually
} else {
    var node = Scene.appendNodeFromAsset(baseAsset);
    DzSleep(0.2);
    Scene.selectNode(node);
}

// If no node was appended, try to use currently selected figure
var selection = Scene.getSelectedNode();
if (!selection) {
    DzApp.log("No figure selected. Please select the target figure in the Scene pane and re-run.");
    throw "No figure selected";
}
var figure = selection;

// -------- Create reference plane with image ----------
try {
    // create a plane primitive
    var plane = DzPrimitive.createPlane(1.0, 1.0); // 1x1 meter plane
    Scene.addItem(plane);
    plane.setLabel("ReferencePlane_Img");
    // apply image as texture to the plane
    var mat = plane.getMaterial();
    if (mat) {
        var imgUri = DzFileUtils.uriFromPath(imgPath);
        var tex = mat.getDiffuseColor().getTexture();
        if (!tex) {
            tex = new DzImage();
            mat.getDiffuseColor().setTexture(tex);
        }
        tex.setFileName(imgUri);
        DzApp.log("Reference image applied to plane: " + imgPath);
    }
    // place plane behind figure and slightly offset
    var nodeTransform = plane.findNode("Transform");
    nodeTransform.setZ(-0.5);
    plane.setDoubleProperty("displayFrustum","true");
} catch (e) {
    DzApp.log("Failed to create reference plane: " + e);
}

// -------- Create camera aligned to ref image ----------
try {
    var camAsset = DzContentMgr.getAssetFromFile("Cameras/Cameras.duf"); // fallback camera asset
    var camNode = Scene.createCamera();
    camNode.setLabel("ReferenceCamera");
    Scene.getViewport().setPrimaryCamera(camNode);
    DzApp.log("Reference camera created.");
} catch (e) {
    DzApp.log("Camera creation failed: " + e);
}

// -------- Load morph JSON (if provided) ----------
var morphs = {}; // default empty
if (jsonPath && fileExists(jsonPath)) {
    try {
        var jsonText = DzFile(jsonPath).readText();
        morphs = JSON.parse(jsonText);
    } catch (e) {
        DzApp.log("Failed to read or parse JSON morph file: " + e);
    }
} else {
    DzApp.log("No JSON morph file provided; script will not apply morphs automatically.");
}

// -------- Apply morphs to figure ----------
function setParamByName(node, name, val) {
    var p = node.getParameterByLabel(name);
    if (!p) p = node.getParameterByName(name);
    if (p) {
        p.setValue(val, true);
        DzApp.log("Set param: " + name + " = " + val);
        return true;
    } else {
        DzApp.log("Param not found: " + name);
        return false;
    }
}

for (var key in morphs) {
    try {
        var value = morphs[key];
        setParamByName(figure, key, value);
    } catch (e) {
        DzApp.log("Error applying morph " + key + ": " + e);
    }
}

// -------- Save Persona preset ----------
try {
    if (savePersonaPath) {
        DzApp.savePersonaPreset(savePersonaPath, figure);
        DzApp.log("Persona saved to: " + savePersonaPath);
    }
} catch (e) {
    DzApp.log("Persona save failed: " + e);
}

// -------- Optional: Export OBJ ----------
try {
    if (exportObjPath) {
        Scene.selectNode(figure);
        DzApp.exec("Export", { "fileName": exportObjPath, "format": "OBJ" });
        DzApp.log("Exported OBJ: " + exportObjPath);
    }
} catch (e) {
    DzApp.log("OBJ export failed: " + e);
}

DzApp.log("Script finished.");
Sample JSON format (send me something like this or I’ll generate it from your photo)
Name the file morph_values.json. This is the exact input the script above expects.

{
  "height": 1.02,
  "headSize": 0.96,
  "faceWidth": 0.12,
  "jawWidth": 0.18,
  "chinLength": 0.05,
  "noseLength": 0.08,
  "noseWidth": 0.04,
  "eyeSpacing": -0.02,
  "eyeSize": 0.05,
  "mouthWidth": -0.03,
  "lipFullness": 0.10,
  "neckThickness": 0.03,
  "chestSize": -0.02,
  "waistScale": -0.06,
  "hipWidth": 0.08,
  "muscleMass": 0.15,
  "legLength": 0.06,
  "armThickness": 0.04
}
Notes:

Morph keys must match the parameter labels or internal names in the figure asset. If a key fails to find a parameter it logs “Param not found”. That’s normal — different character packs use different naming. If you want, paste the parameter names from the figure and I’ll remap automatically.

Values are example ranges. Most morph sliders are 0.0–1.0, but some accept negatives. I’ll tell you if any values look out of range.

How I estimate morph values from your photo (so you know how I’m not guessing wildly)
I’ll use simple proportional measurements from the photo: e.g., head height vs. torso height, shoulder width vs. head width, eye separation relative to head width. Quick mapping examples:

Face width ratio → faceWidth (0.0 neutral → ±0.2 extremes)

Jaw prominence → jawWidth, chinLength

Nose length/width → noseLength, noseWidth

Lip fullness → lipFullness based on vertical lip thickness ratio

Eye spacing → eyeSpacing (negative to pull closer)

Body height vs head height → height and legLength

Shoulder width vs hip width → hipWidth, chestSize

If you want pixel-accurate conversion, I’ll ask you to mark a couple of anchor points on the photograph (top of head, chin, left temple, right temple, shoulder points). But if you upload a clean front-facing photo, I’ll estimate automatically and return a JSON plus a Persona preset to run through the script above.

Limitations & practical gotchas (don’t throw a chair)
I cannot magically recreate 1:1 from a single low-res or heavily angled photo. Front-facing, neutral expression, good lighting gives best results.

DAZ morphs differ by content pack. If your figure pack uses different names, I’ll need either the parameter list or I’ll map best-fit names and tell you which didn’t apply.

For perfect realism you’ll want a secondary pass in Blender for sculpting and retopology. I’ll give you exports ready for that.

Alright, upload the image and say whether you want realistic or stylized. I’ll return:

The filled morph_values.json estimated from the photo.

A Persona preset file (or instructions where to save it) and the command to run the DAZ script above.

Optional: an exported OBJ/FBX ready for Blender/UE5.

Do it. I’ll do the tedious mapping and hand you a working preset so you can stop fiddling and get back to building the prison nightmares.

1) Assumption / scale

If this is wrong for your facility, tell me the real number and I’ll rescale everything correctly.

Scale formula used throughout:

meters_per_pixel = door_real_height_meters / door_pixel_height
All objects’ real-world dimensions = pixel_dimension * meters_per_pixel.

2) Measurement method (what I do and what you can reproduce)
Steps I use to convert video → measurements:

Extract representative frames from the video that show the scene orthogonally or with minimal perspective distortion (front-facing doorway, top of stairs, main hall, cell/cell-gate close-up).

For each frame, annotate (or automatically detect) bounding boxes for key items: doors, walls, gates, bed bunks, toilets, stairs steps, table tops, cups, light fixtures, floor tile corners, etc. Record bounding box coordinates and pixel sizes (width_px, height_px).

For features made of repeated linear elements (cell bars, stair treads, bars in a gate), detect and measure spacing by counting edges or vertical lines (Hough or contour methods) and average spacing in pixels. Convert spacing → meters with meters_per_pixel.

When perspective foreshortening is present, use multiple frames or vanishing-point corrections (if needed) — I can do that automatically with the video if you want full precision.

Produce a top-down grid by measuring distances between known anchors (door corners, floor tiles, or other known elements visible from multiple frames).

Important: the more orthogonal (front-on, side-on) views you supply the higher the accuracy. Close-ups of details (toilets, cups, gates) increase per-object precision.

3) Blender-ready Python (bpy) — parametric generator
This script is ready to copy into Blender’s Text Editor and run (Blender 2.8+).
It’s parametric: fill the door_pixel_height and the pixel bounding boxes in pixel_measurements dictionary, and it will compute meters_per_pixel and create geometry at scale.

Important: this script uses only primitive mesh objects (cubes, cylinders) to approximate the objects. You can refine materials, beveling, or detail later.

# Blender generator for scene from pixel measurements.
# Paste into Blender's Scripting workspace and run (Blender 2.8+).
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------
import bpy
import math

# ---------------------------
# USER INPUT / PIXEL MEASUREMENTS (REPLACE THESE VALUES)
# ---------------------------
# Reference: door real height (meters)
door_real_height_m = 2.032  # assumed / change to your real door height

# The only required numeric item here for initial run is door_pixel_height.
# Replace with the actual pixel height of the door in the chosen frame.
door_pixel_height = 600.0   # <-- REPLACE with measured pixel height from frame

# Example pixel bounding boxes for objects in chosen frame.
# Format: (x, y, width_px, height_px) -- x,y top-left in pixels (frame coordinate system)
# Replace/add as many as you have measured (I will fill them if I run full extraction).
pixel_measurements = {
    "door": (100, 120, 120, 600),          # x,y,w,h (px)  - example placeholder
    "main_wall_left": (0, 0, 300, 800),
    "main_wall_right": (900,0,300,800),
    "floor_area": (300, 600, 600, 200),
    "stairs_group": (400,400,300,200),
    "cell_gate": (650,200,120,480),
    "bunk_1": (200,420,200,80),
    "toilet_1": (780,520,50,60),
    "table_1": (500,520,120,60),
    "cup_table1": (540,500,12,16),
    "light_1": (520,80,36,36),
    # Add or replace measured objects...
}

# Add bar spacing measured in pixels for repeated elements (vertical bars)
bar_measurements_px = {
    "cell_gate_bar_spacing_px": 16.0,   # how many px between bar centers (placeholder)
    "cell_gate_bar_thickness_px": 4.0,
}

# Frame resolution used when measuring pixel boxes
frame_width_px = 1280.0   # replace with your frame width in px
frame_height_px = 720.0   # replace with your frame height in px

# ---------------------------
# AUTOCALCULATED SCALE
# ---------------------------
meters_per_pixel = door_real_height_m / door_pixel_height
print("meters_per_pixel = {:.6f} m/px".format(meters_per_pixel))

# Utility: convert pixel dims to meters (use center coordinates in scene XY plane)
def px_to_m(px):
    return px * meters_per_pixel

# Create simple materials
def mk_mat(name, color):
    mat = bpy.data.materials.new(name)
    mat.diffuse_color = color
    return mat

mat_wall = mk_mat("WallMat", (0.8,0.8,0.8,1))
mat_floor = mk_mat("FloorMat", (0.4,0.4,0.4,1))
mat_metal = mk_mat("Metal", (0.2,0.2,0.2,1))
mat_bed = mk_mat("Bed", (0.6,0.4,0.3,1))
mat_plastic = mk_mat("Plastic", (0.9,0.9,0.9,1))

# Clear scene (optional - comment if you don't want this)
bpy.ops.object.select_all(action='SELECT')
bpy.ops.object.delete(use_global=False)

# Set origin at world center and create floor plane sized to frame px dims
floor_px = pixel_measurements.get("floor_area", (0,0,frame_width_px, frame_height_px))
fx, fy, fw_px, fh_px = floor_px
# Convert to meters
fw_m = px_to_m(fw_px)
fh_m = px_to_m(fh_px)
floor = bpy.ops.mesh.primitive_plane_add(size=1, location=(0,0,0))
floor_obj = bpy.context.active_object
floor_obj.scale = (fw_m/2.0, fh_m/2.0, 1)
floor_obj.name = "Floor"
floor_obj.data.materials.append(mat_floor)

# Create walls (simple cubes extruded up from edges)
def create_wall(name, bbox_px, thickness_m=0.15, height_multiplier=1.0):
    x_px,y_px,w_px,h_px = bbox_px
    width_m = px_to_m(w_px)
    height_m = px_to_m(h_px) * height_multiplier
    # place wall with its center at converted coordinates (we map frame XY -> world X,Y)
    # Frame top-left -> world (x = -frame_width/2 + x_px + w_px/2)
    cx_px = x_px + w_px/2.0
    cy_px = y_px + h_px/2.0
    world_x = (cx_px - frame_width_px/2.0) * meters_per_pixel
    world_y = - (cy_px - frame_height_px/2.0) * meters_per_pixel  # invert Y to Blender coords
    z = height_m/2.0
    bpy.ops.mesh.primitive_cube_add(size=1, location=(world_x, world_y, z))
    obj = bpy.context.active_object
    obj.scale = (width_m/2.0, thickness_m/2.0, height_m/2.0)
    obj.name = name
    obj.data.materials.append(mat_wall)
    return obj

# Example walls
create_wall("MainWallLeft", pixel_measurements["main_wall_left"], thickness_m=0.25)
create_wall("MainWallRight", pixel_measurements["main_wall_right"], thickness_m=0.25)

# Create door as an opening (two door jambs; or a single mesh)
dx,dy,dw_px,dh_px = pixel_measurements["door"]
door_w_m = px_to_m(dw_px)
door_h_m = px_to_m(dh_px)
door_cx_px = dx + dw_px/2.0
door_cy_px = dy + dh_px/2.0
door_world_x = (door_cx_px - frame_width_px/2.0) * meters_per_pixel
door_world_y = - (door_cy_px - frame_height_px/2.0) * meters_per_pixel
# Make a simple door opening by subtracting a box from a wall -- simplified: create door object
bpy.ops.mesh.primitive_cube_add(size=1, location=(door_world_x, door_world_y, door_h_m/2.0))
door_obj = bpy.context.active_object
door_obj.scale = (door_w_m/2.0, 0.1, door_h_m/2.0)
door_obj.name = "Door_approx"
door_obj.data.materials.append(mat_plastic)

# Create a simple cell gate built from vertical bars using bar spacing
gate_px = pixel_measurements.get("cell_gate", None)
if gate_px:
    gx, gy, gw_px, gh_px = gate_px
    gate_world_x = (gx + gw_px/2.0 - frame_width_px/2.0) * meters_per_pixel
    gate_world_y = - ((gy + gh_px/2.0) - frame_height_px/2.0) * meters_per_pixel
    gate_height_m = px_to_m(gh_px)
    gate_width_m = px_to_m(gw_px)
    # generate vertical bars across gate width
    bar_spacing_px = bar_measurements_px.get("cell_gate_bar_spacing_px", 16.0)
    bar_th_px = bar_measurements_px.get("cell_gate_bar_thickness_px", 4.0)
    bar_spacing_m = px_to_m(bar_spacing_px)
    bar_th_m = px_to_m(bar_th_px)
    # number of bars (ensure full coverage)
    n_bars = max(2, int(math.floor(gate_width_m / bar_spacing_m)))
    start_x = gate_world_x - gate_width_m/2.0 + bar_spacing_m/2.0
    for i in range(n_bars):
        bx = start_x + i*bar_spacing_m
        bpy.ops.mesh.primitive_cube_add(size=1, location=(bx, gate_world_y, gate_height_m/2.0))
        bobj = bpy.context.active_object
        bobj.scale = (bar_th_m/2.0, 0.06, gate_height_m/2.0)
        bobj.name = f"Gate_bar_{i}"
        bobj.data.materials.append(mat_metal)

# Create a simple bunk bed (approx)
if "bunk_1" in pixel_measurements:
    bx,by,bw_px,bh_px = pixel_measurements["bunk_1"]
    bunk_w = px_to_m(bw_px)
    bunk_h = px_to_m(bh_px)
    bx_world = (bx + bw_px/2.0 - frame_width_px/2.0) * meters_per_pixel
    by_world = - ((by + bh_px/2.0) - frame_height_px/2.0) * meters_per_pixel
    # create mattress box
    bpy.ops.mesh.primitive_cube_add(size=1, location=(bx_world, by_world, 0.4))
    bed = bpy.context.active_object
    bed.scale = (bunk_w/2.0, px_to_m(0.9*bw_px)/2.0, 0.15) # approximate depth from width
    bed.name = "Bunk_Mattress"
    bed.data.materials.append(mat_bed)

# Create a table and cup (approx)
if "table_1" in pixel_measurements:
    tx,ty,tw_px,th_px = pixel_measurements["table_1"]
    tw = px_to_m(tw_px)
    tx_world = (tx + tw_px/2.0 - frame_width_px/2.0) * meters_per_pixel
    ty_world = - ((ty + th_px/2.0) - frame_height_px/2.0) * meters_per_pixel
    # tabletop
    bpy.ops.mesh.primitive_cube_add(size=1, location=(tx_world, ty_world, 0.9))
    table = bpy.context.active_object
    table.scale = (tw/2.0, px_to_m(th_px)/2.0, 0.04)
    table.name = "Table"
    table.data.materials.append(mat_wall)
    # cup
    if "cup_table1" in pixel_measurements:
        cx,cy,cw_px,ch_px = pixel_measurements["cup_table1"]
        cup_r = px_to_m(cw_px)/2.0
        cup_x_world = (cx + cw_px/2.0 - frame_width_px/2.0) * meters_per_pixel
        cup_y_world = - ((cy + ch_px/2.0) - frame_height_px/2.0) * meters_per_pixel
        bpy.ops.mesh.primitive_cylinder_add(radius=cup_r, depth=px_to_m(ch_px), location=(cup_x_world, cup_y_world, 0.92))
        cup = bpy.context.active_object
        cup.name = "Cup"
        cup.data.materials.append(mat_plastic)

# Create a simple toilet approximation
if "toilet_1" in pixel_measurements:
    tx,ty,tw_px,th_px = pixel_measurements["toilet_1"]
    t_w_m = px_to_m(tw_px)
    t_x_world = (tx + tw_px/2.0 - frame_width_px/2.0) * meters_per_pixel
    t_y_world = - ((ty + th_px/2.0) - frame_height_px/2.0) * meters_per_pixel
    bpy.ops.mesh.primitive_cylinder_add(radius=t_w_m/2.0, depth=px_to_m(th_px)/1.5, location=(t_x_world, t_y_world, 0.45))
    toilet = bpy.context.active_object
    toilet.name = "Toilet"
    toilet.data.materials.append(mat_plastic)

# Create a simple light fixture as sphere
if "light_1" in pixel_measurements:
    lx,ly,lw_px,lh_px = pixel_measurements["light_1"]
    l_r = px_to_m(lw_px)/2.0
    l_x_world = (lx + lw_px/2.0 - frame_width_px/2.0) * meters_per_pixel
    l_y_world = - ((ly + lh_px/2.0) - frame_height_px/2.0) * meters_per_pixel
    bpy.ops.mesh.primitive_uv_sphere_add(radius=l_r, location=(l_x_world, l_y_world, px_to_m(150))) # high ceiling approx
    light = bpy.context.active_object
    light.name = "LightFixture"
    light.data.materials.append(mat_plastic)

print("Scene generation completed. Replace pixel values with measured ones and re-run for accurate scale.")
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------
Notes about the script:

Replace door_pixel_height with the actual measured pixel height of the door in the frame you used.

Replace frame_width_px / frame_height_px with the actual resolution of that frame (e.g., 1920x1080).

Replace the entries in pixel_measurements with actual bounding boxes you measure on the frames.

The script will place objects in the Blender XY plane mapped from frame coordinates (frame top-left → world coordinate transform handled inside).

Bars/gates are instantiated as repeated thin cube bars using bar_measurements_px spacing.

The script produces simple primitives — you can refine shapes, materials, and add bevels later.

4) Exact pixel data I need from you (or I can measure automatically on a retry)
To deliver the final precise measurement list and the exact Blender geometry I will convert pixel units to meters using the door scale. The minimum set of pixel values I need (per frame) is:

door_pixel_height (px) — required (used to compute meters_per_pixel).

For every object: bounding box in px (x, y, width_px, height_px) on the chosen frame(s):

walls (left/right/front) — full extents

stairs group bounding box (and for stair treads: individual tread heights in px or number of treads + vertical extents)

rails/handrails bounding boxes (so I can measure thickness and length in px)

cell gate bounding box + pixel spacing between vertical bars (or sample three adjacent bar center x-coordinates)

toilet bounding box (or close-up)

each bunk mattress bounding box (top & side if available)

pillow/blanket close-ups (if you need those exact sizes)

each table bounding box and cup bounding box(s)

light fixtures bounding box (for diameter)

floor tile size in px (if tiles are present, good to measure)

If you want distances between rooms: coordinates of two anchors visible in different frames (or visible in same frame). Provide pixel coordinates of anchor points.

If you want me to automatically extract those pixel boxes from the uploaded MP4, say so and I will re-run the analysis — I’ll need permission to try again. (The previous attempt timed out — I can retry with different frame choices or chunking.)

5) Example output format I will give (once I have pixel values)
When I convert the pixel data with your confirmed door height, I will produce:

A) A per-object table like:

[Object: Cell Gate]
- Source frame: frame_001.jpg
- Pixel bbox: x=650, y=210, w=120, h=480
- Pixel bar spacing (center-to-center): 16 px
- Computed scale: 0.003387 m/px   <-- example
- Real Height: 480 px * 0.003387 = 1.625 m
- Real Width: 120 px * 0.003387 = 0.406 m
- Bar spacing (real): 16 px * 0.003387 = 0.054 m
- Bar thickness: 4 px -> 0.0135 m
B) A full top-down grid (PNG vector / CSV coordinates) listing every object with x,y coordinates in meters (relative to a chosen origin e.g. door corner), widths, depths, and heights.

C) A Blender Python file (the code above, plus auto-filled pixel values), ready to run to re-create the entire scene as primitives.

- Average run (px) = 40 px => run real = 0.135 m (approx)

8. THE HISTORY OF NYGHTSHADE HOLLOW
This place didn’t just appear one night like a bad dream. It has layers. The kind that smell like mildew and unfiled reports.

1912 — The Founding
Built as the Springfield Industrial Reformatory, a state experiment in “moral labor.” Basically a torture warehouse disguised as an economic initiative.
The original structure had:

A foundry

Textile floors

Basement punishment chambers

None of these were ever officially recorded, but they’re still buried under C-Block.

1946 — The Quiet Shift
After the war, staff shortages led to veterans being hired as guards.
This is when the first whispers of “the Hollow” started.
Harrington Marlowe arrived not long after.
He swears the walls were already screaming when he got here.

1978 — The Blackout Incident
Power went out for 27 minutes.
During that time:

3 inmates disappeared

1 guard went catatonic

Records were burned
No one was charged.
The place got its modern name after this: Nyghtshade Hollow.

1999 — The Modernization
Digital systems installed.
Half the wiring was done wrong.
The cameras blink like they’re morse-coding warnings.

2020s — Present Era
Under Warden Bridget Galletti, things finally stopped collapsing.
Mostly because she’s too stubborn to let the Hollow rot.

22. DEEP HISTORY OF THE BLOCKS
A-Block History
Flooded in 1954.
Half the block had to be rebuilt.
They say bones were found behind the old walls.

B-Block History
Originally meant for juvenile offenders.
Converted after a scandal ended the program.

C-Block History
Built using funds that mysteriously “doubled” overnight.
No one knows who funneled the extra money.
The lights still buzz wrong.

D-Block History
Originally called Sunset Wing.
Harrington remembers when it was brand new.
Now it smells like dust and lost hope.

Bridget Galletti — Warden

Hidden flaw: he keeps the key close because he hopes someone will find out

28. THE FIVE LOST ERAS OF NYGHTSHADE HOLLOW
Every great prison has eras like tree rings. Here are yours.

Era I – The Foundry Years (1912–1938)
Industry-driven punishment.
Brutal.
Quiet.
Rumors of early disappearances already existed.
Blueprints from this era are… wrong.

Era II – The War Return (1940s–1950s)
Veterans hired as guards.
Discipline became military.
This is when the Hollow began reacting to people.

Era III – The Reform Lie (1960s–1978)
A failed modernization that ignored the bones of the building.
Electrical issues start.
The Hollow starts acting “alive.”
Ends with the 1978 blackout.

Era IV – Digital Dysfunction (1980s–2015)
Half-complete upgrades.
New staff culture.
The rise of factions.
Most haunting events documented in this era.

Era V – The Galletti Era (2015–Present)
Bridget brings order.
But she doesn’t know the full truth.
Nobody does.
