**SECTION 4
SECURITY DIVISION LORE (FULL INTERNAL DOSSIER)**
────────────────────────

These are the people who make sure the place doesn’t burn down before lunch. Most are barely holding it together. Some are frighteningly competent. A few should absolutely not be in charge of anything sharper than a paper clip.

1. Captain Rosalind “Roz” Keener
Role: Head of Security
Age: 46
Physical: Tall, wiry, pulled-back silver-streaked hair, eyes that look like she’s survived five timelines.
Personality: Think hyper-disciplined military vet trapped in a bureaucracy she hates but can’t leave.
Backstory:
Roz spent 20 years in the Air Force, then got looped into private security contracting. Two contracts went south, two made her rich, and one left her with tinnitus and a pathological hatred of supervisors. When Maddox Callahan Thatcher offered her full autonomy over Hollow security, she took it.
In-Prison Reputation:
Inmates fear her because she never raises her voice. Officers fear her because she never repeats herself.

Gameplay Hook:
She has a secret project: an abandoned wing called Block E, sealed after a 1993 riot. Roz keeps it off the books. Something down there still moves.

2. Officer Benny “Two-Step” Larkson
Role: Floor Guard, Unit B
Age: 29
Physical: Soft but stout, always sweating, uniforms too tight.
Personality: Completely incompetent in every measurable category but somehow beloved by inmates.
Backstory:
Benny only works at the Hollow because his uncle sits on the state corrections board. He’s terrified of confrontation and tries to defuse situations with jokes. When that fails, he panics. When he panics, he dances. Literally. Two little sidesteps. Hence the name.
In-Prison Reputation:
Inmates protect him out of pity. Officers think he’s a walking scandal.

Gameplay Hook:
If Benny goes missing, the entire block goes into chaos because the inmates assume someone finally killed him…and they want revenge even though they can't stand him.

3. Corporal Sage Draymond
Role: Night-Shift Rover
Age: 38
Physical: Lean, long-legged, buzzcut, dark circled eyes
Personality: Paranoid, hyper-alert, conspiracy theorist with actual instincts
Backstory:
Sage worked in three separate prisons. Each one shut down due to “incidents” that were never publicly explained. He insists something inhuman happened at all three. Hollow hired him because no one else would take night shift in a place with its history.

In-Prison Reputation:
Half the inmates think he’s haunted. The other half think he’s right.

Gameplay Hook:
Sage will sometimes mutter about “the thing in the vents.” He’s not joking. This becomes relevant in late-game.

4. Intake Specialist Janelle Ortega
Role: Handles new arrivals
Age: 32
Physical: Sharp eyeliner, dark curly hair, immaculate uniform
Personality: Sarcastic, overworked, emotionally numb except when she’s caffeinated
Backstory:
Janelle grew up in Springfield, went to MSU for psychology, and somehow ended up doing paperwork at the most haunted prison in the Midwest. She knows every inmate’s full history and uses this knowledge sparingly and strategically.

In-Prison Reputation:
She has a memory like a steel vault. No one lies to her twice.

Gameplay Hook:
Janelle secretly runs a hidden database of inmates’ unofficial traits, which can unlock special branching storylines.

**SECTION 5
PRISON INFRASTRUCTURE LORE (ADVANCED)**
────────────────────────

You wanted every detail? I’m dumping the whole vault. This part reads like a facility manual written by someone who hates their job but still triple-checks everything.

THE LAYOUT (MACRO)
Nyghtshade Hollow operates in a tri-ring layout:

Outer Ring (Containment Belt)
Built in 1974, cracked concrete, rusting razor wire, floodlights that flicker like they’re paid hourly.
Facilities include:
• Perimeter patrol road
• Watchtowers (6 total, Tower 4 is permanently unmanned for “electrical issues”)
• Vehicle entry gate
• Staff lot

Middle Ring (Operations Zone)
All administrative functions.
Includes:
• Security HQ
• Warden’s Office
• Medical / Infirmary
• Chapel
• Laundry
• Workshop
• Commissary

This ring has reinforced walls added after the '93 riot.

Inner Ring (The Core)
Where inmates actually live, suffer, plan, gamble, pray, etc.
Divisions include:
• Block A – General Population
• Block B – High-Risk
• Block C – Work Release
• Block D – Segregation / The Hole
• Block E – Sealed Wing (Roz’s project; “doesn’t exist”)

THE CHAPEL
Built in 1957 when the prison was still a state-run reformatory. Has three strange features:

A stone floor not native to Missouri

An iron door in the basement that predates the building

Windows that don’t accumulate dust

NPC preacher rotates daily depending on faction control.

THE INFIRMARY
Run by Bridget Galletti, artistic angel with a steel backbone.
Originally four rooms. Expanded to ten after a series of “accidents” in the 80s.
Room 6 is always empty. Staff refuse to assign anyone there.

THE WORKSHOP
Home of inmate labor.
Machinery is older than the prison’s corruption.
One machine is chained to the floor because it “turns on by itself.”
Documented complaint, 1989:
“Machine ran a 40-minute cycle with no operator and no power.”

THE HOLE (Block D)
No cameras.
No windows.
No sound except the hum of old plumbing banging through concrete like a heartbeat.
Inmates report hearing whispering.
Roz denies all reports.

SECTION 7
FACTIONS, GANGS, POWER STRUCTURES, AND SHADOW NETWORKS
────────────────────────

Nyghtshade Hollow’s inmate world operates like a city-state inside a concrete ribcage. Every faction has three faces:
• the public story
• the internal truth
• the secrets only the Hollow knows

Here we go.

1. THE RAZOR HOUNDS
Type: Old-school prison gang
Origin: Formed in 1978 during the drought riots
Territory: Block B upper tier, yard benches near Tower 3
Colors: Rust red, bone white tattoos

Leadership
Boss: Carter “Dogma” Vayn
Age 42, built like a tank that’s sad about its life choices.
Serves his third bid. Doesn’t speak unless he’s absolutely sure you deserve the words.

Beliefs
They run on brutality over strategy. They view Nyghtshade Hollow as sacred ground because it's “never broken,” no matter how many riots or lockdowns hit it.

Operations
• Debt collection
• Weapon crafting (shivs, garrotes, ceramics from kitchen scraps)
• Underground fight circuits

Hidden Lore
The Hounds have a map of the sealed Block E, passed down through bosses since the 80s. It’s hand-sketched, meticulous, and marked with a symbol that appears nowhere else in the prison.

2. THE CHALK MEN
Type: Intellectual syndicate
Origin: Started by lifer Harrington Marlowe in 1999
Territory: Block C library tables, chapel corner pews
Colors: Pale gray chalk marks on knuckles

Leadership
Founder: Harrington Marlowe
The old man runs debates like warfare. He remembers everything. Everything.

Beliefs
Power comes from information, not muscle. You control the flow of knowledge, you control the Hollow.

Operations
• Legal help
• Message encryption
• Smuggling coded notes via Bible margins
• Inmate tutoring (literacy, math, history, philosophy)

Hidden Lore
Chalk Men archives include names of staff involved in a 1977 cover-up. Those names match some of the things Corporal Sage Draymond whispers about when he thinks no one's listening.

3. THE SUNCAGE COLLECTIVE
Type: Spiritual faction
Origin: Nothing official. Rumors say it began after a power outage in 1986.
Territory: Chapel basement landing, Rec Yard shadow corners
Colors: Golden thread bracelets

Leadership
Guide: “Mother Finch”
Age unknown. No inmate record exists for her, yet she appears at all ceremonies. Could be hallucination. Could be a real person moving unnoticed. Could be something else.

Beliefs
Light is truth. Darkness is memory. Pain is the bridge.

Operations
• Ritual circles
• Meditative (sometimes dangerous) breathing sessions
• Offering boxes hidden behind chapel pipes
• Strange chants that echo in halls without speakers

Hidden Lore
Members swear they’ve seen the chapel floor glow during storms. Staff brushed it off as wet stone reflecting lightning. They’re wrong.

4. THE UNION OF QUIET HANDS
Type: Craftworkers / labor guild
Origin: Formed unofficially by workshop inmates trying to survive broken machinery
Territory: Workshop, laundry tunnels, storage mezzanines
Colors: Blue thread tied around wrist or ankle

Leadership
Coordinator: Franky Dun (inmate #0248)
Former mechanic, arrested for a robbery that went sideways. Brilliant with tools.

Beliefs
Fix everything. Break nothing unless someone deserves it.

Operations
• Machine repair
• Illicit metalwork
• Custom contraband fabrication
• Supply chain manipulation (laundry is power in any prison)

Hidden Lore
They discovered the workshop machine that “turns itself on” responds to vibrations from Block E. Franky installed a sensor. He didn't tell Roz.

5. THE QUIET 44
Type: Underground assassination cell (NPC faction)
Origin: A rumored contract group dating back to the old reformatory
Territory: None. They’re nomadic shadows.
Colors: None. Their symbol is absence.

Leadership
Unknown. No one claims membership. Some say the group dissolved decades ago. Others say it never dissolved, it just stopped being visible.

Beliefs
No doctrine. Only purpose. They act when the Hollow demands a correction.

Operations
• Silent eliminations
• Resetting power balances
• Removing threats to the prison’s “equilibrium”

Hidden Lore
Sage Draymond once saw “someone” vanish into the ventwork. He still won't talk about that night.

SECTION 9
THE SUPERNATURAL SUBSTRUCTURE OF NYGHTSHADE HOLLOW
────────────────────────

Nyghtshade Hollow didn’t become strange. It started strange.

This section breaks into three parts:

What the prison was built on

What the prison became

What the prison hides that nobody has ever successfully explained

Here we go.

1. WHAT THE PRISON WAS BUILT ON
PRE-PRISON HISTORY (1812–1900)
The land at 1900 W Sunshine St, Springfield, MO 65807 was originally a settlement clearing called The Hollow, named by early surveyors who noted the ground felt “unnaturally hollow beneath the soil.”

Two things happened here before the prison:

A. The Finch Settlement
Small religious community founded in 1812. They believed the land contained a “door to illumination,” described in journals as “the place where light has weight.”

The settlement vanished in winter 1824.
Cabins abandoned.
Cookfires still warm.
Livestock untouched.
No bodies found.

Local rumor: The Finch people “followed the light downward.”

B. Civil War Redoubt, 1864
Union troops fortified the Hollow for three weeks.
Their official logs mention:
“Lights below the ridge.”
“Voices in the earth.”
“Men going missing without breach of line.”

The redoubt was abandoned suddenly. No battle, no record of why.

Commanding officer shot himself seven months later.

2. WHAT THE PRISON BECAME
ORIGINAL REFORMATORY (1901–1948)
Nyghtshade Reformatory was built in 1901 as a “model institution.” Early staff reported:

• unexplained heat blooms
• dust that falls upward in corner rooms
• footsteps in empty hallways
• inmates sleepwalking into the basement stairwell

The basement contained a large iron door that predates every blueprint of the building.
No keyhole.
No hinges.
Always cold.

The reformatory expanded, but the construction crews refused to dig below Level 2 after 1917.

3. THE BLACK YEAR (1948)
This is the biggest buried event.

No public records survive, but internal fragments show:

• 32 prisoners dead
• 9 officers dead
• 1 building wing “sealed permanently”
• all staff replaced within 6 months
• a total media blackout with state signatures

Whatever happened forced the transition from “reformatory” to “correctional facility.”

This is when Block E’s original layout changed.
This is also when the foundation began… shifting.

More on that later.

4. WHAT THE PRISON HIDES NOW
THE FOUNDATION THAT MOVES
The bedrock under the Hollow has been recorded as shifting since the 1950s, always subtly, always downward.

Survey teams recorded the elevation dropping 1.6 inches between 1956 and 1969.

By 2020, total recorded drop: over 4 feet.

And yet, the building hasn’t cracked.
Instead, some corridors… stretch.

5. BLOCK E: THE SEALED WING
The crown jewel of the Hollow’s nightmares.

Official Position
Block E does not exist.

Actual Reality
Block E was first constructed in 1932 as a maximum-restriction testing wing.

By 1972, the state sealed it.

By 1993, prison staff added steel plating over the original doors.

Command said it was due to “structural unsoundness.”
Sage Draymond calls that a lie.
Roz Keener keeps a physical key she never uses.

Behavior of the Block E Zone
• The air pressure fluctuates without climate equipment
• Sound is muffled even before you reach the sealed doors
• Lights flicker in the hallway despite perfect power flow
• A faint hum resonates in the pipes connected to that area

Electrician Alexi Rourke says the wing is still drawing power from a line that “goes nowhere on any map.”

Inmate Reports (unconfirmed):
• footsteps behind solid walls
• laughter during storms
• a low metallic scraping sound that repeats in threes
• seeing someone walk past the sealed door window when the window shouldn't be there

6. THE IRON DOOR
The oldest artifact in the entire facility.

Located in the basement beneath the chapel.
Pre-dates the building.
Pre-dates state records.
Pre-dates everything.

Every attempt to remove it has failed.

Torching it made the flame bend sideways.
Cutting into it dulled three carbide blades.
Charging it with a forklift lifted the forklift instead.

Behind the door is stone, then void.
A void that somehow… exhales.

Unholy Trivia
Bridget Galletti found old medical logs from 1965 that mention inmates complaining of whispering from the chapel basement.

The logs were unsigned.
But the handwriting matches the old warden’s notes.

7. THE “LIGHT BELOW”
Remember the Finch settlement?
The “light of weight”?

It’s still there.

Staff on graveyard shift occasionally report faint golden illumination in vents, floor cracks, or beneath doors.

Not bright. More like a glow under the skin of the building.

Suncage Collective Interpretation:
The light is a “memory of illumination.”
Something the Hollow wants people to see, but not understand.

Scientific Explanation:
There isn’t one. And no one sane is trying to make one.

8. WHEN THE HOLLOW BREATHES
This happens once or twice a year.

Only during thunderstorms.
Only between 1:51 AM and 2:03 AM.

The air shifts inward.
Lights dim.
Pipes vibrate.
Inmates wake up at the exact same moment.
Roz Keener gets out of bed without an alarm.
And Block E’s sealed doors frost over like something behind them is holding its breath.

The phenomenon lasts 12 minutes.

Always 12 minutes.

SECTION 10
THE WARDENS OF NYGHTSHADE HOLLOW
A TIMELINE OF POWER, CORRUPTION, AND QUIET PANIC
────────────────────────

Every warden leaves fingerprints on the Hollow. Some fingerprints are bloody. Some are shaking. Some are carved into the stone like the building wanted to remember them.

Let’s run the timeline.

1. WARDEN ISAIAH FENBROOK
Term: 1901–1917
Legacy: Founder of the reformatory.
Style: Scholarly, moralistic, obsessed with “order through enlightenment.”

What he built
• The chapel
• The basement meditation room (later sealed)
• The foundational doctrine of rehabilitation

Hidden Lore
His journals document “light signatures” beneath the stone and “vibrations that harmonize with prayer.”
He vanished in 1917 without explanation.

His shoes were found at the top of the chapel stairs.
Perfectly placed.
No Fenbrook.

2. WARDEN RUTHEN MARKLE
Term: 1917–1932
Legacy: Industrialization of the prison.
Style: Brutal efficiency.

What he built
• Workshop wing
• Laundry tunnels
• First version of Block E (then called Section Epsilon)

Hidden Lore
Markle demanded a basement excavation in 1921.
The workers refused after two days.
All six claimed they heard chanting from under the dirt.

All six were transferred out of state within the year.
None worked corrections again.

3. WARDEN MAEVE ROTHWELL
Term: 1932–1948
Legacy: Architect of the old maximum-restriction block.
Style: Cold, calculating, believed fear was the greatest reformer.

What she built
• Block E’s reinforced sublevels
• The sealed maintenance corridor
• Hardline isolation policies

Hidden Lore
Rothwell is the one tied to the Black Year event.
Internal memos say she “opened something she shouldn’t have” in the sublevels.
Her body was never recovered.
Her desk remained locked until 1974.

Inside:
A key.
A map.
A journal with the last line scratched so hard it cut the paper:
“The light is not ours.”

4. WARDEN CALVIN BRIGGS
Term: 1949–1962
Legacy: Damage control.
Style: Quiet, nervous, constantly requesting budget increases for “structural interventions.”

What he built
• Reinforced Block E plates
• Restriction of basement access
• Multiple chapel restorations
(Which is hilarious, because the chapel kept aging faster than the rest of the building.)

Hidden Lore
Recorded 41 unexplained injuries among staff related to “invisible hazards.”
Never explained.
Resigned after waking up in the basement stairwell with no memory of walking there.

5. WARDEN LYLE HUNTERS
Term: 1962–1977
Legacy: Crackdowns and cover-ups.
Style: Militaristic.

What he built
• First CCTV system
• Staff barracks upgrades
• Purge of “occult rumors”

Hidden Lore
His notes describe the iron door as “the mouth of the building.”
He tried sealing it with a concrete wall.
Next morning the wall had bowed outward like something behind it pushed.

He retired.
Banned all conversation about the basement for twenty years.

6. WARDEN ELOISE VARLEY
Term: 1977–1997
Legacy: Modernization guru.
Style: Corporate, bottom-line thinker.

What she built
• New infirmary
• Staff lounge
• Redesigned visitation wing

Hidden Lore
In 1986, she received a handwritten letter:
“Stop digging.”
The letter was signed “Finch.”
No inmate or staff member had that name.

Security footage showed the envelope sliding under her office door with no one visible.

She ordered Block E welded shut the next day.

7. WARDEN JONATHAN GARETH
Term: 1997–2014
Legacy: The era of silence.
Style: Paranoid, controlling, eerily calm.

What he built
• New segregation tier
• Electronic lock system
• Unexplained “restricted archive” room

Hidden Lore
He was obsessed with the prison’s electrical grid.
Claimed the whole building was acting like “a conductor.”

His final year, he slept in his office because he believed something was “climbing upward” from below the chapel.

Died of a heart attack in his chair.
Eyes still open.
Security footage glitched at 2:03 AM.

8. WARDEN BRIDGET GALLETTI
Term: 2014–Present
Legacy: Stability in chaos.
Style: Methodical, emotionally aware, protective of staff.

What she built
• Modern medical wing
• Staff–inmate wellness programs
• Systematic cleanup of Gareth’s missing-file problem

Hidden Lore
Bridget discovered something terrifying in the old archives:
All warden succession orders match the same date range—
every transition for 124 years happened within the same 30-day window.

She also found a note taped to the underside of her desk drawer:
“Keep the chapel lights on during storms.”
Unsigned.

And she obeys it.
Always.

9. THE UNOFFICIAL, NEVER-RECORDED 9th WARDEN
Rumors say there was a warden between Rothwell and Briggs.
No name.
No files.
Only a single photograph in an old hallway that keeps showing a different face depending on the day.

Sage Draymond refuses to walk past it.

Roz Keener tilts it slightly every time she goes by, as if the picture annoys her.

And inmates swear the figure in the photo moves an inch every year.

The lens glass is warm to the touch.

CHAPTER 11: DEEP STRUCTURE OF THE HOLLOW
The architectural bones, secret guts, and the parts the state pretends don’t exist.

11.1 Main Layout Overview
Nyghtshade Hollow is designed like someone mashed a high school, a warehouse, and a Cold War bunker together.
Key zones:

A-Block
Standard housing. Fights every Tuesday. Sometimes Wednesday if morale is low.

B-Block
Protective custody. Half the population are snitches, the other half are terrified they’ll be mistaken for snitches.

C-Block (The Maw)
High-security. Thirty cells, reinforced doors, acoustic damping.
The place hums at night. Nobody knows why.

The Yard
Concrete playground of chaos. Only safe if you mind your business, your shoelaces, and your mouth.

The Warden’s Tower
Maddox Callahan Thatcher’s roost. Reinforced windows. Extra locks. Looks down on everything like a king watching a kingdom he hates.

Medical Wing
Run by Bridget Galletti. Smells like antiseptic, paint thinner, and regret.

Workshop Row
Machine shop, woodshop, and the metal fabrication bay.
Steel Sons territory. Do not touch the wrong toolbox.

Laundry & Library
The Dustbound Kings’ crossroads.
Laundry is for deals.
Library is for secrets.

The Chapel
Father Vale’s sanctuary.
Crosses, candles, and an uneasy peace you can feel in your ribs.

────────────────────────────────────────

11.2 Underground Levels
You wanted full canon. Here’s the spooky part executives usually pretend doesn’t exist.

Sub-Level 1 (S1): The Tunnels
Concrete maintenance corridors.
Old pipes.
Bad lighting.
Legend says a previous inmate population used these for escape attempts in the 1970s. Most died.

NPCs whisper about shadows that move wrong down here.
No one investigates.
Not even Kessler.

Sub-Level 2 (S2): The Archive Vault
Massive, climate-controlled records room.
Shelves loaded with inmate files going back a century.
Half the files are missing pages.
Some files are redacted so heavily they're just black rectangles.

Father Vale is the only civilian with a master key.
Kessler hates that.
Bridget says she’s seen Vale cry after reading things down there.

Sub-Level 3 (S3): The Old Block
A fully abandoned cell block from before the modernization.
Peeling paint.
Rusted bars.
Electronics dead.
Echoes carry strangely.

Maddox doesn’t allow anyone down here.
Even guards avoid it.
It’s rumored this is where he goes when he “needs quiet.”

Because every good prison needs its ecology of misery.

Vent hums that shift in pitch at night

Metal dust from Workshop Row

✅ 1. Why Move to Unreal Engine 5.5?
Here are the true reasons UE5.5 is chosen for AAA (Epic, CDPR, Kojima Productions, Ninja Theory, etc.):

UE5.5 Core Strengths
Feature	Unity	Unreal Engine 5.5
Nanite (virtualized geometry)	❌ No equivalent	✅ Yes — handles billions of triangles
Lumen (real-time GI)	❌ No full GI system	✅ Yes — dynamic global illumination
World Partition	✔ But less advanced	✅ Yes — built for large streaming worlds
Built-in Cinematics (Sequencer)	✔ Timeline exists	✅ Native film-quality, full NLE
Blueprints	✔ Visual scripting (Bolt)	✅ More integrated, engine-native
Virtual Production	❌ Limited	✅ Yes — industry standard
UE5.5 is chosen for AAA because it is:

Feature-complete out of the box

Production-proven at cinematic scales

Optimized for large teams and multi-department pipelines

Built for film, animation, virtual production, and high-end realtime 3D

✅ MODULE 1 — UE5.5 GAMEPLAY BASICS
(Mapped to Unity equivalents)

➤ 1. Actors (Unity: GameObjects)
Everything in your UE level is an Actor.
Actors can have components, just like Unity GameObjects.

➤ 2. Pawn (Unity: Controllable Object)
A Pawn = an Actor that can receive input.

➤ 3. Character (Unity: CharacterController + Animator)
A Character = Pawn + movement logic + capsule + animation system.

➤ 4. Blueprint Classes (Unity: Prefabs + Scripts combined)
Blueprints =

Prefab

Script (MonoBehaviour)

Inspector
…all in one asset.

➤ 5. Components (Unity: Components)
Identical in concept, but Unreal encourages composition > inheritance.

➤ 6. Events (Unity: Update, Start, Awake)
Unreal avoids Update spam.

Instead, you use:

BeginPlay (Start/Awake)

Tick (Update, but use sparingly)

Input Actions (instead of polling input)

Delegates (Unity events / C# delegates)

✅ MODULE 2 — UNITY → UNREAL CONVERSION
✔ Object Lifecycle Mapping
Unity	Unreal
Awake	OnConstruction / PreInitializeComponents
Start	BeginPlay
Update	Tick (avoid when possible)
FixedUpdate	Physics Substepping
LateUpdate	Tick with specific ordering
✔ Prefabs → Blueprint Classes
Blueprints include:

Meshes

Materials

Variables

Logic

Events

Child Blueprints (Unity nested prefabs equivalent)

✔ ScriptableObjects → DataAssets / DataTables
Use DataAssets for configuration.
Use DataTables for large dataset management.

✔ Addressables → Unreal Asset Manager + Primary Assets
✅ MODULE 3 — NANITE & LUMEN TRAINING
UE5.5’s two biggest next-gen features.

🟪 NANITE (Virtualized Geometry)
Think of Nanite as a “no-more-LODs” system.

What Nanite gives you:
✔ No LOD creation
✔ Billions of polygons
✔ Film-level models instantly usable
✔ Higher performance than LOD meshes

UE5.5 Nanite workflow:
Import a high-poly mesh (.fbx or .obj)

Enable Nanite in the mesh settings

Place it in the world

No further optimization required

🟧 LUMEN (Dynamic GI + Reflections)
Lumen = real-time global illumination + reflections.

What Lumen replaces:
Old System	Lumen
Lightmaps	❌ not needed
Reflection probes	❌ not needed
Baked GI	❌ not needed
Post-process AO hacks	❌ not needed
Lumen setup:
Project Settings → Enable Lumen

Use Directional Light + Sky Light

Add a Post Process Volume

Enable Lumen Reflections + GI

That’s it.

✅ MODULE 4 — CINEMATICS / SEQUENCER
Sequencer is Unity Timeline + Cinemachine + Editor Cameras
rolled into one.

Sequencer Capabilities:
✔ Keyframe animation
✔ Multi-camera editing
✔ Audio tracks
✔ Facial animation (Metahuman)
✔ Camera cuts track
✔ Lighting animation
✔ Material/parameter animation
✔ Render cinematics to video

Typical workflow:
Add a CineCameraActor

Create Level Sequence

Animate camera transforms

Add Camera Cuts track

Render viewport → movie

✅ MODULE 5 — C++ VS C# (FAST COMPARISON)
C# in Unity
Managed

Garbage collected

Lightweight

Great iteration speed

Easy to read

C++ in Unreal
Native

Memory control

Exposes properties to Blueprints

Faster at runtime

Steeper learning curve

Key translation:
Concept	Unity C#	Unreal C++
Class	public class Player : MonoBehaviour	UCLASS() class APlayerCharacter : public ACharacter
Variable exposed to Inspector	public int Health;	UPROPERTY(EditAnywhere) int32 Health;
Update	void Update()	virtual void Tick(float DeltaSeconds) override;
BUT:
You write far less C++ gameplay code than Unity C# code.

Blueprints handle a ton.

    Allow double jump logic

✅ MODULE 2 — FULL C++ IMPLEMENTATIONS
Clean Unreal C++ written in studio style.

Character Header
UCLASS()
class AMyCharacter : public ACharacter
{
    GENERATED_BODY()

public:
    AMyCharacter();

protected:
    virtual void BeginPlay() override;
    virtual void SetupPlayerInputComponent(UInputComponent* PlayerInputComponent) override;

    void Move(const FInputActionValue& Value);
    void Look(const FInputActionValue& Value);
    void Fire();
    void Dodge();

private:
    UPROPERTY(EditAnywhere)
    USpringArmComponent* CameraBoom;

    UPROPERTY(EditAnywhere)
    UCameraComponent* FollowCamera;

    UPROPERTY(EditAnywhere)
    TSubclassOf<AActor> ProjectileClass;
};
Character CPP
AMyCharacter::AMyCharacter()
{
    PrimaryActorTick.bCanEverTick = true;

    CameraBoom = CreateDefaultSubobject<USpringArmComponent>(TEXT("CameraBoom"));
    CameraBoom->SetupAttachment(RootComponent);

    FollowCamera = CreateDefaultSubobject<UCameraComponent>(TEXT("FollowCamera"));
    FollowCamera->SetupAttachment(CameraBoom);
}

void AMyCharacter::BeginPlay()
{
    Super::BeginPlay();
}

void AMyCharacter::SetupPlayerInputComponent(UInputComponent* PlayerInputComponent)
{
    // Enhanced Input bindings imported here
}

void AMyCharacter::Move(const FInputActionValue& Value)
{
    FVector2D Movement = Value.Get<FVector2D>();
    if (Controller)
    {
        const FRotator YawRotation(0, Controller->GetControlRotation().Yaw, 0);
        const FVector Forward = FRotationMatrix(YawRotation).GetUnitAxis(EAxis::X);
        const FVector Right = FRotationMatrix(YawRotation).GetUnitAxis(EAxis::Y);

        AddMovementInput(Forward, Movement.Y);
        AddMovementInput(Right, Movement.X);
    }
}

void AMyCharacter::Look(const FInputActionValue& Value)
{
    FVector2D LookAxis = Value.Get<FVector2D>();
    AddControllerYawInput(LookAxis.X);
    AddControllerPitchInput(LookAxis.Y);
}

void AMyCharacter::Fire()
{
    if (ProjectileClass)
    {
        FVector MuzzleLocation = GetMesh()->GetSocketLocation("Muzzle");
        FRotator MuzzleRotation = GetControlRotation();

        GetWorld()->SpawnActor<AActor>(ProjectileClass, MuzzleLocation, MuzzleRotation);
    }
}

🎮 PLAYER MOVEMENT
EventGraph
Event BeginPlay
→ Add Mapping Context (EnhancedInput)

Event Move(InputActionValue)
→ Get Control Rotation
→ Break Rotator (Yaw only)
→ Make Rotator (Yaw)
→ Get Forward Vector / Right Vector
→ Add Movement Input

Event Look(InputActionValue)
→ Add Controller Yaw Input
→ Add Controller Pitch Input
🎥 CAMERA SYSTEM
CameraBoom (SpringArm)
→ Attach to Root
→ Set TargetArmLength = 350
→ Enable Camera Lag (Speed 12)
→ Enable Camera Rotation Lag (Speed 15)

FollowCamera
→ Attach to CameraBoom
🗡 COMBAT COMPONENT (Blueprint Flow)
OnAttack input:
InputAction Attack
→ Branch (bIsAttacking)
    False:
        → Play Montage("Attack1")
        → Set bIsAttacking = True

        AnimNotify on HitFrame:
            → Activate Hitbox

        AnimNotify on ComboWindow:
            → Allow Next Combo

        AnimNotify End:
            → Set bIsAttacking = False
🌀 DODGE / ROLL
InputAction Dodge
→ If (Stamina ≥ Cost)
    → Subtract Stamina
    → Play Montage("DodgeRoll")
    → Launch Character (Impulse)
    → Set Invulnerable = True
    → Delay(0.45)
    → Set Invulnerable = False
🎯 TARGET LOCK
InputAction LockOn
→ SphereTraceMultiByChannel (Radius: 2500)
→ Filter by "Enemy" tag
→ Find Closest by Distance & Angle
→ Set CurrentTarget
→ Rotate Player Toward Target
→ CameraBoom → Enable TargetOffsetTracking
🧠 AI ENEMY BEHAVIOR TREE
Root Selector
Selector
 ├── Sequence: Attack Player
 │    → Check "CanAttack"
 │    → MoveTo(Player)
 │    → Play Attack Montage
 │
 ├── Sequence: Investigate Noise
 │    → Check "HeardNoise"
 │    → MoveTo(LastNoiseLocation)
 │    → Wait(3)
 │
 └── Patrol
      → Go To Random Patrol Point
      → Wait(2)
🧩 EQS (Environment Query System)
Generator: Points: Grid
Test 1: Distance to Player (prefer 400–600)
Test 2: Visibility (Line of Sight)
Test 3: Avoid Other Enemies (min_spacing = 200)
This gives smart flanking and spacing behavior.

🎮 PLAYER CHARACTER C++ (FINAL VERSION)
MyCharacter.h
UCLASS()
class AMyCharacter : public ACharacter
{
    GENERATED_BODY()

public:
    AMyCharacter();

protected:
    virtual void BeginPlay() override;
    virtual void SetupPlayerInputComponent(UInputComponent* PlayerInputComponent) override;

private:
    void Move(const FInputActionValue& Value);
    void Look(const FInputActionValue& Value);
    void Attack();
    void Dodge();
    void LockOn();

    UPROPERTY(EditAnywhere, Category="Camera")
    USpringArmComponent* CameraBoom;

    UPROPERTY(EditAnywhere, Category="Camera")
    UCameraComponent* FollowCamera;

    UPROPERTY(EditAnywhere, Category="Combat")
    TSubclassOf<class AWeapon> WeaponClass;

    UPROPERTY(VisibleAnywhere)
    class UCombatComponent* CombatComp;

    UPROPERTY(VisibleAnywhere)
    class UTargetLockComponent* TargetLockComp;

    UPROPERTY(VisibleAnywhere)
    class UAbilitySystemComponent* AbilitySystem;

    UPROPERTY(VisibleAnywhere)
    class UAttributeSetBase* Attributes;
};
MyCharacter.cpp
AMyCharacter::AMyCharacter()
{
    PrimaryActorTick.bCanEverTick = true;

    CameraBoom = CreateDefaultSubobject<USpringArmComponent>("CameraBoom");
    CameraBoom->SetupAttachment(RootComponent);

    FollowCamera = CreateDefaultSubobject<UCameraComponent>("FollowCamera");
    FollowCamera->SetupAttachment(CameraBoom);

    CombatComp = CreateDefaultSubobject<UCombatComponent>("CombatComp");
    TargetLockComp = CreateDefaultSubobject<UTargetLockComponent>("TargetLockComp");

    AbilitySystem = CreateDefaultSubobject<UAbilitySystemComponent>("AbilitySystem");
    Attributes = CreateDefaultSubobject<UAttributeSetBase>("Attributes");
}

void AMyCharacter::BeginPlay()
{
    Super::BeginPlay();
}

void AMyCharacter::SetupPlayerInputComponent(UInputComponent* PlayerInputComponent)
{
    // Enhanced Input mapping context added here...
}

void AMyCharacter::Move(const FInputActionValue& Value)
{
    FVector2D Input = Value.Get<FVector2D>();
    if (Controller)
    {
        FRotator YawRot(0, Controller->GetControlRotation().Yaw, 0);
        FVector Forward = FRotationMatrix(YawRot).GetUnitAxis(EAxis::X);
        FVector Right   = FRotationMatrix(YawRot).GetUnitAxis(EAxis::Y);

        AddMovementInput(Forward, Input.Y);
        AddMovementInput(Right,   Input.X);
    }
}

void AMyCharacter::Look(const FInputActionValue& Value)
{
    FVector2D Look = Value.Get<FVector2D>();
    AddControllerYawInput(Look.X);
    AddControllerPitchInput(Look.Y);
}

void AMyCharacter::Attack()
{
    CombatComp->StartAttack();
}

void AMyCharacter::Dodge()
{
    CombatComp->PerformDodge();
}

void AMyCharacter::LockOn()
{
    TargetLockComp->ToggleLockOn();
}

Thought for 10s
Nice — awesome reference. I’ll give you a complete, actionable, step-by-step plan to reproduce that exact glossy black + red prison interior (and the matching exterior pieces) in Unity using HDRP (best for filmic reflective surfaces) and Visual Studio (any modern version, your VS 2026 will work for scripting). I’ll include editor settings, art/UV tips, HDRP material/shader settings, lighting, reflections, and C# scripts for doors/gates so you can assemble a 1:1 match.

I’ll assume you want real-time filmic quality (mirror floor with red veins, emissive highlights, ceiling strip lights, matte black walls with red trim, metal doors/gates/cell doors, reflective puddles/scuffs). If you want baked global illumination or ray tracing, I’ll note it where useful.

1) Project & Render Pipeline Setup (HDRP — recommended)
Create a new Unity project → High Definition RP (HDRP) template.

Install packages (Package Manager):

HDRP (matching Unity version)

Shader Graph

Visual Effect Graph (optional for dust/particles)

ProBuilder (for rapid blockout)

In Edit → Project Settings → Graphics, assign the HDRP Asset created by the template.

HDRP Asset settings to enable (suggested):

Lit Shader Mode: Use Material Quality = High

Reflections: Enable Screen Space Reflection (SSR) and Planar Reflection support (planar reflection may require the "Planar Reflection Probe" package or custom script)

Shadows: High resolution, enable contact shadows for crisp contact with reflective floor

Decals: Enable if you plan decals for scuffs/paint chips

(Optional, higher quality) If your hardware supports it and you want the absolute best reflections, enable HDRP Ray Tracing and configure in HDRP asset — but performance cost is high.

2) Scene Blockout & Geometry
Use ProBuilder or model in Blender/Maya. Keep UVs clean.

Scene pieces to create:

Long corridor core volume (walls, floor, ceiling)

Ceiling recess with rectangular linear light panels

Two or more side doors + double doors at far end (same style as reference)

Cell doors / bars / gate meshes (thin vertical bars with frame)

Baseboard / lower red trim and middle red stripe (separate geometry or use decal/material)

Exterior façades/outer walls (if you want outside look) — keep same materials

Modeling tips:

Make floor tiles as large square tiles to mimic reflections (can be one mesh with UVs tiled).

Make the red stripe as either:

a separate narrow mesh inset into the wall surface (best for precise 100% visual match), or

paintable via decal or splat on material.

Create the doors and gate as separate prefabs, with pivot at hinge for rotation, or sliding axis for gates.

3) Floor Material (Marble / Mirror with red veins)
Use HDRP Lit shader (or Shader Graph to author custom). Key: highly smooth (low roughness), strong normal map, emissive red vein map subtly glowing.

Assets needed:

Albedo/base color: near-black marble texture

Red vein mask (grayscale for mixing emissive red) or a red veins color texture with alpha

Normal map for marble surface

Roughness (or smoothness) map – low roughness where glossy

Ambient occlusion map (optional)

(Optional) Height map for micro detail

Material parameters (HDRP Lit) — start with these values and tweak visually:

Surface Type: Opaque

Workflow: Metallic (but set Metallic = 0)

Base Color (Albedo): RGB ~ (10,10,10) (very dark) multiplied by marble base texture

Smoothness / Roughness: Smoothness = 0.92 – 0.98 (gives mirror-like)

Normal Map: assign strong normal (Intensity ~ 0.6–1.0)

Clear Coat: Enable → ClearCoat = 1.0, ClearCoat Smoothness = 0.98 (adds realistic glossy overlay)

Specular Color: white (leave default)

Emissive: use the red vein mask * emissive color (R:1.0, G:0, B:0), Emissive Intensity small (1.0–3.0) so veins glow slightly for color bleed in reflections

Tiling: set UV tiling so veins tile realistically (e.g., 1–2 per floor tile)

Double-Sided GI: No (floor only needs one side)

Enable SSR, Planar reflection contributions in the material (HDRP supports these).

Planar reflection: place a Planar Reflection Probe along the floor to get perfect mirror-like real-time reflections. If you have long corridor, align a large planar reflection for the center area. Also place Reflection Probes around the scene for static objects.

Decals for scuffs/wear: Use Decal Projectors for subtle wear, dirt near walls/doors. HDRP decal system works well.

4) Wall Material (Matte Black with Red Stripe)
Create one master wall material that supports a red stripe mask.

Approaches:

Make the red stripe a separate mesh with its own emissive/opaque red material. (This yields exact control and no shader blending issues.)

Or create a single shader with a mask texture: black base + red stripe blended via mask.

Shader Graph (if using single material):

BaseColor = Lerp(BlackColor, RedColor, StripeMask)

Smoothness: Black area ~ 0.1 (matte), Red stripe ~ 0.6–0.9 depending on reference (looks satin to glossy)

Add a small normal map for subtle plaster irregularities.

Suggested values:

Black wall Smoothness: 0.05–0.15

Red stripe Smoothness: 0.6–0.9 (slightly glossy)

Emissive for stripe: none (just bright albedo) unless you want neon glow.

5) Ceiling & Lights
The reference shows recessed rectangular strip lights.

Best approach (HDRP):

Create rectangular Area Lights (box area) inside the ceiling recess. HDRP area lights produce soft realistic lighting and reflections on the glossy floor.

Set lights to Intensity around 3000–10000 lumens (tweak based on exposure) and set color temperature to ~4000K–4500K (neutral white).

Use Multiple small rectangular area lights in a row to produce the repeating pattern.

Additional settings:

Use Volumetric Fog (Exponential Height Fog with volumetric enabled) for subtle God rays if desired. Keep subtle to preserve the crisp industrial look.

6) Doors, Gates, Cell Doors — Materials & Mechanics
Materials:

Metal material for doors/gates: metallic = 1.0, smoothness = 0.25–0.45 (slightly reflective), normal map for scratches. Use a red-painted metal variant for the red elements on doors.

Use edgewear / rust decals near bottom and hinges for realism.

Mechanics (C# scripts): two examples below:

A) Hinge door (swing)

// DoorHinge.cs
using UnityEngine;
public class DoorHinge : MonoBehaviour
{
    public Transform hinge;
    public float openAngle = 90f;
    public float openSpeed = 2f;
    bool isOpen = false;
    Quaternion closedRot, openedRot;

    void Start(){
        closedRot = hinge.localRotation;
        openedRot = closedRot * Quaternion.Euler(0f, openAngle, 0f);
    }
    public void ToggleDoor(){
        isOpen = !isOpen;
        StopAllCoroutines();
        StartCoroutine(RotateDoor(isOpen ? openedRot : closedRot));
    }
    System.Collections.IEnumerator RotateDoor(Quaternion target){
        while(Quaternion.Angle(hinge.localRotation, target) > 0.5f){
            hinge.localRotation = Quaternion.Slerp(hinge.localRotation, target, Time.deltaTime * openSpeed);
            yield return null;
        }
        hinge.localRotation = target;
    }
}
Attach to door prefab; hook ToggleDoor to a trigger or interact key.

B) Sliding gate (translate)

// SlidingGate.cs
using UnityEngine;
public class SlidingGate : MonoBehaviour
{
    public Transform gate;
    public Vector3 closedPos;
    public Vector3 openPos;
    public float speed = 3f;
    bool isOpen = false;

    void Start(){
        closedPos = gate.localPosition;
        // compute openPos manually or via inspector
    }
    public void ToggleGate(){
        isOpen = !isOpen;
        StopAllCoroutines();
        StartCoroutine(MoveGate(isOpen ? openPos : closedPos));
    }
    System.Collections.IEnumerator MoveGate(Vector3 target){
        while(Vector3.Distance(gate.localPosition, target) > 0.01f){
            gate.localPosition = Vector3.Lerp(gate.localPosition, target, Time.deltaTime * speed);
            yield return null;
        }
        gate.localPosition = target;
    }
}
Interaction: add colliders and triggers for door activation and use the Player script to call ToggleDoor on keypress when in range.

7) Reflections & Polished Look
Planar Reflection (floor): use a PlanarReflectionProbe for near-perfect mirror. Place it just above the floor plane with its capture settings matching camera. For long corridors, you may need multiple probes tiled or a large one that covers the area.

Screen Space Reflection (SSR): enable in HDRP and tweak thickness & quality. SSR handles reflections of dynamic objects.

Reflection Probes: for static objects and corridors farther from planar reflection.

Contact Shadows: enable for crisp shadow grounding on the floor.

8) Post Processing / Color Grading / Filmic Look
Use HDRP Volume + Post Process overrides:

Exposure: Manual mode; set exposure to match image brightness (target EV ~ -1 to 0 depending).

Tone Mapping: ACES (filmic) or Neutral with slight contrast. ACES is great for cinematic.

Bloom: low intensity, threshold tuned so only lights cause bloom (not the red veins unless you want that).

Vignette: subtle (0.05–0.15)

Chromatic Aberration: subtle or off

Grain/Film: very subtle; helps realism.

9) Scuffs, Wetness & Small Details
Use Decals for scuffs and puddles (projected decals on floor). Use a puddle decal with a normal map and low roughness to simulate wet reflective patches.

Add subtle normal detail tiling across floor to break perfect mirror, especially near walls and doors.

Use emissive bleed under the red stripe if you want subtle color bounce in reflections.

10) Exterior (matching look)
Use same black & red materials on outer walls / gates to match interior.

Use slightly rougher floor material outside.

Add exterior area lights or emissive strips to match the same red motif.

11) Performance & Optimization
Use LOD Group for distant objects (even if floor is glossy).

Bake lightmaps only for static props if you prefer static GI (in HDRP you can mix realtime Lumen and baked GI).

Keep planar reflections limited to key areas (they’re expensive). For long levels, use reflection probes or SSR to supplement.

12) Quick checklist to replicate EXACT LOOK (copy/paste)
HDRP project, enable SSR & Planar Reflection.

Model corridor: floor tiles, walls, recessed ceiling with rectangular lights, side doors, final double doors, cell gates.

Floor material: HDRP Lit + ClearCoat + Smoothness 0.95 + red vein emissive mask (intensity ~1–3).

Walls: matte black (Smoothness 0.05) + separate red stripe mesh (albedo red #C22020) with higher smoothness.

Ceiling lights: rectangular Area Lights, intensity tuned, color ~4200K.

Place Planar Reflection Probe aligned to floor. Add reflection probes for depth.

Add Decals: scuffs, puddles, paint chips.

Add post-process volume with ACES tone mapping, Bloom, manual exposure.

Add door/gate scripts (provided above) and animation notifies for sound/sparks.

Tweak until the reflections and stripe alignment match the photo.

13) Example material parameter values to paste into your notes
Floor Base Color (RGBA): (0.03, 0.03, 0.03, 1)

Floor Smoothness: 0.96

Floor ClearCoat: 1.0, ClearCoatSmoothness: 0.98

Floor Emissive Color: (0.9, 0.05, 0.05) × EmissiveMask (intensity 1.5)

Wall Base Color: (0.02, 0.02, 0.02), Smoothness: 0.08

Red Stripe Color: (0.75, 0.07, 0.07), Smoothness: 0.7

14) Scripting & Interaction: Player side quick snippet to call door when pressing E
// PlayerInteract.cs
using UnityEngine;
public class PlayerInteract : MonoBehaviour
{
    public float interactRange = 2f;
    void Update(){
        if(Input.GetKeyDown(KeyCode.E)){
            Ray ray = new Ray(transform.position + Vector3.up * 1.2f, transform.forward);
            if(Physics.Raycast(ray, out RaycastHit hit, interactRange)){
                var hinge = hit.collider.GetComponentInParent<DoorHinge>();
                if(hinge) hinge.ToggleDoor();
                var gate = hit.collider.GetComponentInParent<SlidingGate>();
                if(gate) gate.ToggleGate();
            }
        }
    }
}

How to use (quick):

Run the Python script (below) on your machine. It produces two PNGs: floor_albedo.png and floor_emissive.png in the script folder.

Put those PNGs into your Unity project under Assets/Resources/Textures/ (create the folders).

Create a new HDRP Unity project (HDRP template). Install packages as required.

Add the C# scripts to Assets/Scripts/ in Unity.

In a new empty Scene, add an empty GameObject and attach ProceduralPrisonBuilder.cs. Press Play (or run its Editor method) — it will build the corridor and assign materials. Doors/gates are created with prefabs you can interact with (press E to toggle near them).

Part A — Python: generate red-veined marble textures
Save this as generate_marble.py and run with Python 3 (needs numpy and Pillow). It produces floor_albedo.png and floor_emissive.png (2048x2048).

# generate_marble.py
# Requirements: numpy, pillow
# pip install numpy pillow

import numpy as np
from PIL import Image, ImageFilter
import math
import os

OUTDIR = os.path.abspath(".")
SIZE = 2048

def fbm_noise(shape, octaves=5, lacunarity=2.0, gain=0.5, seed=0):
    np.random.seed(seed)
    h, w = shape
    base = np.random.rand(h, w).astype(np.float32) * 2 - 1
    total = np.zeros_like(base)
    amplitude = 1.0
    frequency = 1.0
    for _ in range(octaves):
        # simple scaled random noise sampled at lower resolution
        sx = max(2, int(h / frequency))
        sy = max(2, int(w / frequency))
        small = np.random.rand(sx, sy).astype(np.float32) * 2 - 1
        # scale up small to full size
        big = np.array(Image.fromarray(((small + 1) * 127.5).astype(np.uint8)).resize((w,h), Image.BILINEAR)).astype(np.float32)/127.5 - 1
        total += big * amplitude
        amplitude *= gain
        frequency *= lacunarity
    # normalize
    total = (total - total.min()) / (total.max() - total.min() + 1e-9)
    return total

def marble_base(h, w, scale=6.0, turbulence=5.0, seed=0):
    # create a base gradient and apply sinusoidal marble with noise
    x = np.linspace(0, scale * math.pi, w, dtype=np.float32)
    y = np.linspace(0, scale * math.pi, h, dtype=np.float32)
    X, Y = np.meshgrid(x, y)
    fbm = fbm_noise((h,w), octaves=6, seed=seed)
    # marble pattern: sin(X + turbulence*noise)
    marble = np.sin(X + turbulence * fbm * 2.0)
    # convert -1..1 to 0..1
    marble = (marble + 1.0) * 0.5
    return marble, fbm

def create_floor_maps(size=SIZE, seed=42):
    h = w = size
    marble, fbm = marble_base(h, w, scale=8.0, turbulence=6.5, seed=seed)
    # base color: very dark rock, veins will be bright red
    base_dark = np.zeros((h,w,3), dtype=np.uint8)
    base_dark[...,0] = (marble * 12).astype(np.uint8)   # R very low
    base_dark[...,1] = (marble * 12).astype(np.uint8)
    base_dark[...,2] = (marble * 12).astype(np.uint8)
    # create vein mask by thresholding marble + high-frequency fbm
    veins = (marble * 1.5 + (fbm - 0.5) * 0.8)
    veins = (veins - veins.min()) / (veins.max() - veins.min() + 1e-9)
    # sharpen veins
    veins_img = Image.fromarray((veins * 255).astype(np.uint8)).filter(ImageFilter.UnsharpMask(radius=2, percent=200, threshold=3))
    veins = np.array(veins_img).astype(np.float32)/255.0
    # threshold to create thin veins
    veins = (veins > 0.60).astype(np.float32) * (veins**1.2)
    # blend veins into color (red/orange)
    red_layer = np.zeros((h,w,3), dtype=np.uint8)
    red_layer[...,0] = (veins * 255).astype(np.uint8)
    red_layer[...,1] = (veins * 70).astype(np.uint8)
    red_layer[...,2] = (veins * 40).astype(np.uint8)
    # combine with base dark and a bit of gloss lines
    albedo = np.clip(base_dark.astype(np.int32) + red_layer.astype(np.int32), 0, 255).astype(np.uint8)

    # Emissive map: keep veins bright red (intensity map)
    emissive = np.zeros((h,w,3), dtype=np.uint8)
    emissive[...,0] = (veins * 220).astype(np.uint8)
    emissive[...,1] = (veins * 40).astype(np.uint8)
    emissive[...,2] = (veins * 10).astype(np.uint8)

    # subtle tile seams/lines (simulate tile edges reflection)
    seam = np.ones((h,w), dtype=np.float32)
    # create faint rectangular grid lines
    tile_px = int(size / 6)
    for tx in range(0, w, tile_px):
        seam[:, max(0,tx-1):min(w,tx+1)] *= 0.6
    for ty in range(0, h, tile_px):
        seam[max(0,ty-1):min(h,ty+1), :] *= 0.6
    # apply seam to albedo (darken lines a bit)
    albedo = (albedo.astype(np.float32) * seam[...,None]).astype(np.uint8)

    # save images
    Image.fromarray(albedo).save(os.path.join(OUTDIR, "floor_albedo.png"))
    Image.fromarray(emissive).save(os.path.join(OUTDIR, "floor_emissive.png"))
    print("Saved floor_albedo.png and floor_emissive.png to", OUTDIR)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    create_floor_maps()
Notes:

This produces a stylized red-veined marble and an emissive mask you can use as the emissive texture for the veins.

You can re-run with different seeds or size to vary the pattern.

Part B — Unity C# scripts
Create these files in Assets/Scripts/ in your Unity project.

1) ProceduralPrisonBuilder.cs
This MonoBehaviour will construct the corridor geometry, create materials (using the HDRP Lit shader if available), set textures from Resources/Textures, add lights, and place simple reflection probes. Attach to an empty GameObject and run in the Editor (or Play).

// ProceduralPrisonBuilder.cs
using UnityEngine;
#if UNITY_EDITOR
using UnityEditor;
#endif

[ExecuteInEditMode]
public class ProceduralPrisonBuilder : MonoBehaviour
{
    [Header("Corridor Dimensions")]
    public float length = 40f;
    public float width = 10f;
    public float height = 5f;
    public int floorTilesAcross = 6;

    [Header("Materials")]
    public Texture2D floorAlbedo;
    public Texture2D floorEmissive;
    public Color wallColor = new Color(0.02f,0.02f,0.02f);
    public Color stripeColor = new Color(0.75f,0.07f,0.07f);

    [Header("Lights")]
    public Color lightColor = Color.white;
    public float areaLightIntensity = 6500f;
    public int ceilingLightsCount = 8;

    private GameObject floorObj, leftWall, rightWall, ceiling;

    void Start()
    {
        // Only run in editor or play
        Build();
    }

    public void Build()
    {
        ClearChildren();

        // Create floor
        floorObj = GameObject.CreatePrimitive(PrimitiveType.Plane);
        floorObj.name = "Floor";
        floorObj.transform.parent = this.transform;
        floorObj.transform.localScale = new Vector3(width / 10f, 1f, length / 10f); // plane is 10x10 by default
        floorObj.transform.localPosition = new Vector3(0f, 0f, length/2f - length/2f);

        // Create walls
        leftWall = GameObject.CreatePrimitive(PrimitiveType.Cube);
        leftWall.name = "LeftWall";
        leftWall.transform.parent = this.transform;
        leftWall.transform.localScale = new Vector3(0.2f, height, length);
        leftWall.transform.localPosition = new Vector3(-width/2f, height/2f, length/2f);

        rightWall = GameObject.CreatePrimitive(PrimitiveType.Cube);
        rightWall.name = "RightWall";
        rightWall.transform.parent = this.transform;
        rightWall.transform.localScale = new Vector3(0.2f, height, length);
        rightWall.transform.localPosition = new Vector3(width/2f, height/2f, length/2f);

        // Ceiling
        ceiling = GameObject.CreatePrimitive(PrimitiveType.Cube);
        ceiling.name = "Ceiling";
        ceiling.transform.parent = this.transform;
        ceiling.transform.localScale = new Vector3(width, 0.2f, length);
        ceiling.transform.localPosition = new Vector3(0f, height, length/2f);

        // Create red stripe meshes (as thin extruded boxes)
        CreateStripe(leftWall.transform.localPosition, true);
        CreateStripe(rightWall.transform.localPosition, false);
        CreateBaseboard(leftWall.transform.localPosition, true);
        CreateBaseboard(rightWall.transform.localPosition, false);

        // Material setup
        var floorMat = CreateFloorMaterial();
        var wallMat = CreateWallMaterial(false);
        var stripeMat = CreateWallMaterial(true);

        AssignMaterial(floorObj, floorMat);
        AssignMaterial(leftWall, wallMat);
        AssignMaterial(rightWall, wallMat);
        // ceiling same as walls but slightly different smoothness
        AssignMaterial(ceiling, wallMat);

        // Assign stripe materials to stripe objects
        foreach (Transform t in transform)
        {
            if (t.name.StartsWith("Stripe") || t.name.StartsWith("Baseboard"))
                AssignMaterial(t.gameObject, stripeMat);
        }

        // Ceiling rectangular lights row
        CreateCeilingLights();

        // Reflection probe near floor center
        CreateReflectionProbe();

        // Add two doors (near left and right sides) and double doors at far end
        CreateDoors();

        Debug.Log("Prison corridor built (ProceduralPrisonBuilder).");
    }

    void ClearChildren()
    {
        var children = new System.Collections.Generic.List<GameObject>();
        foreach (Transform t in transform) children.Add(t.gameObject);
        foreach (var c in children) { #if UNITY_EDITOR
                if (!Application.isPlaying) DestroyImmediate(c);
                else Destroy(c);
            #else
                Destroy(c);
            #endif
        }
    }

    Material CreateFloorMaterial()
    {
        Shader hdrp = Shader.Find("HDRP/Lit");
        Material mat;
        if (hdrp != null)
        {
            mat = new Material(hdrp);
            if (floorAlbedo) mat.SetTexture("_BaseColorMap", floorAlbedo);
            if (floorEmissive) mat.SetTexture("_EmissiveColorMap", floorEmissive);
            // common HDRP properties; user may adjust if names differ in your HDRP version
            mat.SetColor("_BaseColor", Color.black);
            mat.SetFloat("_Smoothness", 0.96f);
            mat.SetFloat("_ClearCoat", 1.0f);
            mat.EnableKeyword("_CLEARCOAT_ON");
            mat.SetColor("_EmissiveColor", Color.red * 0.8f);
        }
        else
        {
            mat = new Material(Shader.Find("Standard"));
            if (floorAlbedo) mat.SetTexture("_MainTex", floorAlbedo);
            mat.SetColor("_Color", Color.black);
            mat.SetFloat("_Glossiness", 0.95f);
            // Emissive map assign:
            if (floorEmissive)
            {
                mat.SetTexture("_EmissionMap", floorEmissive);
                mat.EnableKeyword("_EMISSION");
                mat.SetColor("_EmissionColor", Color.red * 0.6f);
            }
        }
        return mat;
    }

    Material CreateWallMaterial(bool isStripe)
    {
        Shader hdrp = Shader.Find("HDRP/Lit");
        Material mat;
        if (hdrp != null)
        {
            mat = new Material(hdrp);
            if (isStripe) mat.SetColor("_BaseColor", stripeColor);
            else mat.SetColor("_BaseColor", wallColor);
            mat.SetFloat("_Smoothness", isStripe ? 0.7f : 0.08f);
        }
        else
        {
            mat = new Material(Shader.Find("Standard"));
            mat.SetColor("_Color", isStripe ? stripeColor : wallColor);
            mat.SetFloat("_Glossiness", isStripe ? 0.6f : 0.08f);
        }
        return mat;
    }

    void AssignMaterial(GameObject go, Material mat)
    {
        var rend = go.GetComponent<Renderer>();
        if (rend != null) rend.sharedMaterial = mat;
    }

    void CreateStripe(Vector3 wallPos, bool left)
    {
        var stripe = GameObject.CreatePrimitive(PrimitiveType.Cube);
        stripe.name = left ? "Stripe_Left" : "Stripe_Right";
        stripe.transform.parent = this.transform;
        float stripeHeight = height * 0.55f;
        stripe.transform.localScale = new Vector3(0.1f, 0.15f * height, length);
        float x = left ? -width/2f + 0.06f : width/2f - 0.06f;
        stripe.transform.localPosition = new Vector3(x, stripeHeight, length/2f);
    }

    void CreateBaseboard(Vector3 wallPos, bool left)
    {
        var baseb = GameObject.CreatePrimitive(PrimitiveType.Cube);
        baseb.name = left ? "Baseboard_Left" : "Baseboard_Right";
        baseb.transform.parent = this.transform;
        baseb.transform.localScale = new Vector3(0.1f, 0.08f * height, length);
        float x = left ? -width/2f + 0.06f : width/2f - 0.06f;
        baseb.transform.localPosition = new Vector3(x, 0.04f * height, length/2f);
    }

    void CreateCeilingLights()
    {
        // create a series of rectangular area lights (works in HDRP)
        float spacing = length / (ceilingLightsCount + 1);
        for (int i = 0; i < ceilingLightsCount; i++)
        {
            GameObject lightGO = new GameObject("CeilingLight_" + i);
            lightGO.transform.parent = this.transform;
            Light l = lightGO.AddComponent<Light>();
            l.type = LightType.Rectangle;
            l.color = lightColor;
            l.intensity = areaLightIntensity;
            l.range = 20f;
            // size: use rectangular area dims by setting shape via component in HDRP if available
            lightGO.transform.localRotation = Quaternion.identity;
            float z = (i + 1) * spacing;
            lightGO.transform.localPosition = new Vector3(0f, height - 0.18f, z);
        }
    }

    void CreateReflectionProbe()
    {
        GameObject rp = new GameObject("ReflectionProbe_Main");
        rp.transform.parent = this.transform;
        rp.transform.localPosition = new Vector3(0f, 1f, length/2f);
        ReflectionProbe probe = rp.AddComponent<ReflectionProbe>();
        probe.mode = UnityEngine.Rendering.ReflectionProbeMode.Realtime;
        probe.refreshMode = UnityEngine.Rendering.ReflectionProbeRefreshMode.EveryFrame;
        probe.size = new Vector3(width, height, length);
        probe.intensity = 1f;
    }

    void CreateDoors()
    {
        // Create two simple hinge doors at mid corridor sides and double door at far end
        float doorY = 1.0f;
        float doorZLeft = length * 0.25f;
        float doorZRight = length * 0.25f;
        CreateHingeDoor(new Vector3(-width/2f + 0.2f, doorY, doorZLeft), true, "SideDoorLeft");
        CreateHingeDoor(new Vector3(width/2f - 0.2f, doorY, doorZRight), false, "SideDoorRight");
        CreateDoubleDoors(new Vector3(0f, 1f, length - 1.5f));
    }

    void CreateHingeDoor(Vector3 pos, bool openOutwards, string name)
    {
        GameObject root = new GameObject(name);
        root.transform.parent = this.transform;
        root.transform.localPosition = pos;
        GameObject door = GameObject.CreatePrimitive(PrimitiveType.Cube);
        door.name = "DoorBody";
        door.transform.parent = root.transform;
        door.transform.localScale = new Vector3(0.08f, 2f, 1.0f);
        door.transform.localPosition = new Vector3(openOutwards ? 0.04f : -0.04f, 0f, 0f);
        // Add hinge script
        var hingeScript = root.AddComponent<DoorHinge>();
        hingeScript.hinge = door.transform;
        hingeScript.openAngle = openOutwards ? -90f : 90f;
        hingeScript.openSpeed = 6f;
    }

    void CreateDoubleDoors(Vector3 pos)
    {
        GameObject root = new GameObject("DoubleDoors");
        root.transform.parent = this.transform;
        root.transform.localPosition = pos;
        // left half
        GameObject left = GameObject.CreatePrimitive(PrimitiveType.Cube);
        left.name = "LeftDoor";
        left.transform.parent = root.transform;
        left.transform.localScale = new Vector3(0.5f, 2.2f, 0.18f);
        left.transform.localPosition = new Vector3(-0.5f, 0f, 0f);
        // right half
        GameObject right = GameObject.CreatePrimitive(PrimitiveType.Cube);
        right.name = "RightDoor";
        right.transform.parent = root.transform;
        right.transform.localScale = new Vector3(0.5f, 2.2f, 0.18f);
        right.transform.localPosition = new Vector3(0.5f, 0f, 0f);
        // convert to sliding gates for far end
        var leftGate = root.AddComponent<SlidingGate>();
        leftGate.gate = left.transform;
        leftGate.closedPos = left.transform.localPosition;
        leftGate.openPos = left.transform.localPosition + new Vector3(-1.2f,0f,0f);
        leftGate.speed = 3f;
        var rightGate = root.AddComponent<SlidingGate>();
        rightGate.gate = right.transform;
        rightGate.closedPos = right.transform.localPosition;
        rightGate.openPos = right.transform.localPosition + new Vector3(1.2f,0f,0f);
        rightGate.speed = 3f;
    }

#if UNITY_EDITOR
    [CustomEditor(typeof(ProceduralPrisonBuilder))]
    public class ProceduralPrisonBuilderEditor : Editor
    {
        public override void OnInspectorGUI()
        {
            DrawDefaultInspector();
            ProceduralPrisonBuilder builder = (ProceduralPrisonBuilder)target;
            if (GUILayout.Button("Build Corridor"))
            {
                builder.Build();
                EditorUtility.SetDirty(builder);
            }
        }
    }
#endif
}
Notes:

The script tries to use HDRP/Lit shader when available; property names vary slightly between HDRP versions — if your HDRP version uses different property names, open the material in the inspector and tweak settings.

Place the textures generated by Python into Assets/Resources/Textures/floor_albedo.png & .../floor_emissive.png and assign them to the ProceduralPrisonBuilder component fields (or leave the script to default and assign via inspector).

2) DoorHinge.cs (interactive hinge door)
// DoorHinge.cs
using UnityEngine;
public class DoorHinge : MonoBehaviour
{
    public Transform hinge;
    public float openAngle = 90f;
    public float openSpeed = 2f;
    bool isOpen = false;
    Quaternion closedRot, openedRot;

    void Start()
    {
        if (hinge == null) hinge = transform.GetChild(0);
        closedRot = hinge.localRotation;
        openedRot = closedRot * Quaternion.Euler(0f, openAngle, 0f);
    }

    public void ToggleDoor()
    {
        isOpen = !isOpen;
        StopAllCoroutines();
        StartCoroutine(RotateDoor(isOpen ? openedRot : closedRot));
    }

    System.Collections.IEnumerator RotateDoor(Quaternion target)
    {
        while (Quaternion.Angle(hinge.localRotation, target) > 0.5f)
        {
            hinge.localRotation = Quaternion.Slerp(hinge.localRotation, target, Time.deltaTime * openSpeed);
            yield return null;
        }
        hinge.localRotation = target;
    }
}
3) SlidingGate.cs (sliding doors/gates)
// SlidingGate.cs
using UnityEngine;
public class SlidingGate : MonoBehaviour
{
    public Transform gate;
    public Vector3 closedPos;
    public Vector3 openPos;
    public float speed = 3f;
    bool isOpen = false;

    void Start()
    {
        if (gate == null && transform.childCount > 0) gate = transform.GetChild(0);
        closedPos = gate.localPosition;
    }

    public void ToggleGate()
    {
        isOpen = !isOpen;
        StopAllCoroutines();
        StartCoroutine(MoveGate(isOpen ? openPos : closedPos));
    }

    System.Collections.IEnumerator MoveGate(Vector3 target)
    {
        while (Vector3.Distance(gate.localPosition, target) > 0.01f)
        {
            gate.localPosition = Vector3.Lerp(gate.localPosition, target, Time.deltaTime * speed);
            yield return null;
        }
        gate.localPosition = target;
    }
}
4) PlayerInteract.cs (press E to toggle doors/gates when close)
// PlayerInteract.cs
using UnityEngine;
public class PlayerInteract : MonoBehaviour
{
    public float interactRange = 2.5f;
    public KeyCode interactKey = KeyCode.E;

    void Update()
    {
        if (Input.GetKeyDown(interactKey))
        {
            Ray ray = new Ray(transform.position + Vector3.up * 1.2f, transform.forward);
            if (Physics.Raycast(ray, out RaycastHit hit, interactRange))
            {
                var hinge = hit.collider.GetComponentInParent<DoorHinge>();
                if (hinge) hinge.ToggleDoor();
                var gate = hit.collider.GetComponentInParent<SlidingGate>();
                if (gate) gate.ToggleGate();
            }
        }
    }
}
Quick installation checklist (step-by-step)
Create HDRP project and open scene.

Run the Python script and copy floor_albedo.png and floor_emissive.png into Assets/Resources/Textures/.

Add the C# scripts into Assets/Scripts/.

In the Unity Editor create an empty GameObject PrisonBuilder. Add component ProceduralPrisonBuilder.

In the inspector either drag the generated textures to floorAlbedo and floorEmissive fields, or leave and assign later.

Click Build Corridor (button appears in inspector) — the corridor will be generated with walls, stripes, floor, lights, doors, and a reflection probe.

Add a Player object (e.g., the default ThirdPersonController or a capsule with PlayerInteract attached) so you can move near doors and press E to toggle them.

Tweaks & polishing
HDRP: After building, create a custom Material from the generated one and tweak Clear Coat, Smoothness, Emissive intensity until the floor matches the mirror-like photo.

Planar reflections: HDRP Planar Reflection gives the true mirror look — if you have the Planar Reflection Probe component, replace the standard ReflectionProbe with it (package dependent).

Decals: Add scuff decals near the walls for realism.

Post-processing: Use a global Volume with ACES tonemapping and a small Bloom for lights.

✅ PYTHON SCRIPT — SCAN ALL ASSETS & PRINT EVERYTHING

Run inside Unity:

import UnityEditor
import UnityEngine
import os
import json

def get_asset_info(asset_path):
    """Collects all available information about an asset."""
    info = {}

    # Basic metadata
    info["path"] = asset_path
    info["guid"] = UnityEditor.AssetDatabase.AssetPathToGUID(asset_path)
    info["type"] = str(UnityEditor.AssetDatabase.GetMainAssetTypeAtPath(asset_path))
    info["name"] = os.path.basename(asset_path)

    # File info
    real_path = UnityEditor.AssetDatabase.GetAssetPathFromTextMetaFilePath(asset_path)
    if os.path.exists(real_path):
        info["file_size_kb"] = round(os.path.getsize(real_path) / 1024, 2)
    else:
        info["file_size_kb"] = None

    # Labels
    info["labels"] = list(UnityEditor.AssetDatabase.GetLabels(
        UnityEditor.AssetDatabase.LoadAssetAtPath(asset_path, UnityEngine.Object)
    ))

    # Dependencies
    deps = UnityEditor.AssetDatabase.GetDependencies(asset_path, True)
    info["dependencies"] = [d for d in deps if d != asset_path]

    # Import settings
    importer = UnityEditor.AssetImporter.GetAtPath(asset_path)
    if importer:
        settings = {}
        for prop in dir(importer):
            if not prop.startswith("_"):
                try:
                    value = getattr(importer, prop)
                    if isinstance(value, (str, int, float, bool)):
                        settings[prop] = value
                except:
                    pass
        info["import_settings"] = settings
    else:
        info["import_settings"] = {}

    return info


def scan_all_assets(output_json=True):
    print("📦 SCANNING ALL ASSETS IN PROJECT...")
    
    asset_paths = UnityEditor.AssetDatabase.GetAllAssetPaths()
    project_assets = [a for a in asset_paths if "Assets/" in a]

    results = []

    for i, asset in enumerate(project_assets):
        print(f"[{i+1}/{len(project_assets)}] Scanning: {asset}")
        info = get_asset_info(asset)
        results.append(info)

    if output_json:
        out_path = "Assets/asset_database_dump.json"
        with open(out_path, "w") as f:
            json.dump(results, f, indent=4)
        print(f"\n✅ Asset info exported to: {out_path}")

    print("\n✨ Done.")
    return results


# Run the scan
scan_all_assets()

# Kill Unity processes

# Remove Unity EditorPrefs

$editorPrefs2 = "$env:APPDATA\Unity\Editor\EditorPrefs.txt"

Remove-Item $editorPrefs2 -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue

# Remove Unity Logs

$log2 = "$env:LOCALAPPDATA\Unity\Editor\Editor-prev.log"

Remove-Item $log1 -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue
Remove-Item $log2 -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue

# Remove Device Bindings (Profiler + Player connection cache)
Write-Host "Removing device connection cache..."
$unityCache = "$env:LOCALAPPDATA\Unity\Editor"
Get-ChildItem $unityCache -Filter "*.json" -Recurse -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue | Remove-Item -Force

# Clear Unity HUB device list
Write-Host "Clearing Unity Hub device mappings..."
$hubPath = "$env:APPDATA\UnityHub"
Remove-Item "$hubPath\devices.json" -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue
Remove-Item "$hubPath\default-device.json" -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue

Write-Host ""
Write-Host "=== COMPLETE ==="
Write-Host "Unity device connections have been fully reset."
Write-Host "Restart Unity now."

# Remove Unity Hub cache
echo "Clearing Unity Hub device mappings..."

rm -f ~/Library/Application\ Support/UnityHub/default-device.json

echo ""
echo "=== COMPLETE ==="
echo "Unity device connections have been fully reset."
echo "Restart Unity now."
